```text
     >> each pair is generated by the OWNERSHIP pattern, not by
        temperament, and each epoch ends when the forces outgrow it

  SABINE on the FRENCH REVOLUTION: it was a POLITICAL revolution --
     it altered the superstructure to fit a base that had ALREADY
     changed. The bourgeoisie was economically dominant BEFORE 1789.
     >> that is why a political revolution is the COMPLETION of an
        economic change, not its cause
```

### 1. Base and superstructure

Production is treated as the most important human activity, so the organisation of production conditions everything else. The base has two halves that must not be collapsed: **forces of production** = **means of production + labour power**, and **relations of production** = the **pattern of economic ownership**. The 1859 Preface states that the relations constitute the real economic structure **on which rises a legal and political superstructure** — law, state, politics, morality, religion, art, philosophy.

Religion is the printed illustration of how the superstructure works: it is **the sob of the oppressed creature, the heart of a heartless world** and **the opium of the people**. ⚠️ The formulation is two-sided — consolation that also reconciles — and reproducing only the second half misrepresents the passage.

### 2. The mechanism, which is the examinable core

The **fetters** passage supplies the engine. Forces of production develop; at some point the existing relations of production become **fetters** on their further development; **then begins an epoch of social revolution**. 🔑 Three features are worth naming. The contradiction is **internal** (law two, Lesson 11). The onset is **abrupt** after gradual accumulation (law one). And the driver is **technique**, as the printed line states: **the hand-mill gives you society with the feudal lord, the steam-mill society with the industrial capitalist**.

### 3. The four epochs and Sabine's correction

The sequence is **primitive communism** (no private ownership, no classes) → **ancient slave-owning** (master/slave) → **medieval feudal** (lord/serf) → **modern capitalist** (bourgeoisie/proletariat). ⚠️ Each contending pair is generated by the ownership pattern and not by anyone's intentions, which is what makes the theory structural rather than moralising.

**Sabine** supplies the correction that most improves an answer: the **French Revolution (1789)** was a **political** revolution — it altered the superstructure to fit a base that had **already** changed, since the bourgeoisie was economically dominant before the political event. 🔑 Deploy this as the general proposition that a political revolution **completes** an economic transformation rather than causing it. It also explains why Lesson 13 can claim the proletarian revolution is different in kind.

### Deployment in GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay

- **GS-I and GS-II.** On stems about why social orders change or persist, base-and-superstructure gives a causal hypothesis and the fetters passage gives its timing; the Sabine correction prevents the common error of treating political events as first causes.
- **GS-IV.** The superstructure claim is the sharpest statement of the worry that moral vocabulary tracks material interest — essential to any question about whose values an institution embodies.
- **Essay.** The hand-mill line is a compact thesis for prompts on technology and society, provided the determinism is then qualified.

**Answer framework — the structural explanation, in four moves.** Separate the two halves of the base, because an answer that says "economics determines politics" has not stated the theory → run the fetters mechanism to generate the timing → place the case in the four-epoch sequence or name the contending pair the stem implies → qualify with Sabine's correction, so the political event is read as completion rather than cause. ⚠️ A 20-mark "relevance" demand needs this qualification twice: once to show the theory's power and once to bound it.

Revision notes:

- **Dialectical** materialism is the **philosophical** basis; **historical** materialism the **empirical** one.
- **Forces of production** = **means of production + labour power**; **relations of production** = the **pattern of economic ownership**.
- **1859 Preface:** the relations are the real economic structure **on which rises a legal and political superstructure**.
- Superstructure contents: law, state, politics, morality, religion, art, philosophy.
- Religion: **the sob of the oppressed creature, the heart of a heartless world** and **the opium of the people** — consolation that reconciles. ⚠️ Both halves.
- **The fetters passage:** relations become fetters on developing forces → **Then begins an epoch of social revolution**.
- **The hand-mill gives you society with the feudal lord, the steam-mill society with the industrial capitalist.**
- Four epochs: **primitive communism · ancient slave-owning (master/slave) · medieval feudal (lord/serf) · modern capitalist (bourgeoisie/proletariat)**.
- 🔑 **Sabine:** the **French Revolution (1789)** was a **political** revolution completing an economic change already accomplished.
- The *Outline of Historical Materialism* chart summarises the whole apparatus.
- ⚠️ "Economics determines politics" is not the theory; the two halves of the base and the fetters mechanism are.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain historical materialism as a mechanism, and state the correction Sabine's reading of the French Revolution supplies.

**Model answer:** It applies the dialectic to recorded history, so it is the **empirical** counterpart of dialectical materialism's **philosophical** basis. Production is the primary human activity, and the base has two halves: **forces of production**, which are the **means of production plus labour power**, and **relations of production**, which are the **pattern of economic ownership**. The 1859 Preface holds that the relations constitute the real economic structure **on which rises a legal and political superstructure** — law, state, politics, morality, religion, art and philosophy — with religion described as **the sob of the oppressed creature, the heart of a heartless world** and **the opium of the people**, consolation that also reconciles. The mechanism is the **fetters** passage: forces of production develop, existing relations become fetters on them, and **then begins an epoch of social revolution**. Technique drives it, as in the line that **the hand-mill gives you society with the feudal lord, the steam-mill society with the industrial capitalist**. The sequence runs **primitive communism**, **ancient slave-owning** with master and slave, **medieval feudal** with lord and serf, and **modern capitalist** with bourgeoisie and proletariat, each pair generated by the ownership pattern rather than by intentions. ⚠️ **Sabine's** correction is the discriminating addition: the **French Revolution (1789)** was a **political** revolution, altering the superstructure to match a base that had **already** changed, because the bourgeoisie was economically dominant before the political event. 🔑 Generalised, that means a political revolution **completes** an economic transformation rather than causing it — which is why the chapter can claim at the next stage that the proletarian revolution would be different in kind.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not reduce the theory to "economics determines politics". That formulation omits both halves of the base and the fetters mechanism, and it cannot explain timing — why a given order persists for centuries and then breaks. It also invites the opposite error of treating political events as first causes, which Sabine's reading of 1789 expressly corrects.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "A political revolution registers an economic change; it does not produce one." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (within 150 words):** The proposition is **Sabine's** reading generalised, and the chapter supplies both its support and its limit. **Support.** Change originates in the base: as forces of production develop, existing relations become **fetters**, and **then begins an epoch of social revolution** — with technique as driver, since **the hand-mill gives you society with the feudal lord, the steam-mill society with the industrial capitalist**. On that account the **French Revolution (1789)** was **political**: it altered the superstructure to fit a base already transformed, the bourgeoisie being economically dominant before the event. **Limit.** The superstructure is not inert; law, state and ideology reproduce the relations of production, so political action can accelerate, delay or distort the registration. ⚠️ And the chapter itself claims the proletarian case differs in kind, being a revolution of the majority against the minority. Verdict: accurate as a corrective against voluntarism, overstated as a general law.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks: 3 + 3 + 2 + 2):** 3 for the fetters mechanism stated with the two halves of the base distinguished, since "economics causes politics" cannot support the proposition; 3 for the 1789 case used as **evidence** for the registration claim rather than narrated; 2 for a genuine limit — superstructural reproduction, or the claimed difference of the proletarian case — rather than a generic "it is too deterministic"; 2 for a verdict that grades the proposition as a corrective rather than endorsing or rejecting it outright. Deduct 2 for quoting only **the opium of the people** without the consoling half of the passage, and 2 for narrating revolutionary history instead of examining the causal claim.

## Lesson 13 — The theory of revolution, and what makes the proletarian one different

Progress: 13 / 23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Revolution as the necessary form of significant change, ideology as legitimation, the majority-against-minority argument and its finality claim, and Mao's permanent revolution

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Gauba, Ch.24, PDF pp.548–550 (printed 529–531) for the midwife and lever formulations, the *Class Struggle* and *Ideology* boxes, the Manifesto close, the Lenin epigraph and Mao's permanent revolution; Basic owner §4; Advanced owner §§5–6.
CA search: Covered by the single bounded topic-level pass of 3 October 2026; ❌ not repeated per lesson.
CA found: None usable for this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

This lesson completes the revolutionary case so that Lesson 14 can refute a real position rather than a caricature. Its most examinable feature is the argument for **finality** — the claim that one particular revolution ends the series — because that is the claim Popper identifies as unfalsifiable and Mao rejects from inside the tradition.

```text
  FROM MECHANISM TO NECESSITY TO FINALITY              PDF 548-550

  STEP 1  change accumulates slowly, then synthesises suddenly, so
          'any significant social change ... is ALWAYS THE PRODUCT OF
          A REVOLUTION'
          >> 'REVOLUTION IS THE INDISPENSABLE MIDWIFE OF SOCIAL CHANGE'

  STEP 2  why not reform? because of WHO HOLDS POWER
          CLASS STRUGGLE box -- the antagonism generated by ownership
          IDEOLOGY box -- the dominant ideology LEGITIMISES the
          existing order, so consent is available to the rulers
          the dominant class NEVER parts with power until FORCED
          >> 'SOCIAL REVOLUTION IS THE NECESSARY LEVER OF SOCIAL CHANGE'

  STEP 3  the precedent: the FRENCH REVOLUTION 1789 -- the capitalist
          system's own founding overthrow. A system born of revolution
          cannot consistently declare revolution illegitimate.

  STEP 4  WHY THE PROLETARIAN REVOLUTION IS DIFFERENT -- the arithmetic
          every earlier revolution: a MINORITY replaces a MINORITY
             -> exploitation is TRANSFERRED, not ended
          proletarian revolution: the MAJORITY against the MINORITY
             -> no class remains to be exploited
             -> therefore it is THE FINAL REVOLUTION
          and socialism blossoms into communism WITHOUT A FURTHER
          REVOLUTION: 'to each according to his WORK' gives way to
          'to each according to his NEED'
          MANIFESTO's closing declaration supplies the call to action

  STEP 5  THE STATE  (Lenin epigraph)
          'WHILE THE STATE EXISTS, THERE CAN BE NO FREEDOM. WHEN THERE
           IS FREEDOM THERE WILL BE NO STATE'

  STEP 6  THE DISSENT FROM INSIDE  (Mao: PERMANENT REVOLUTION)
          contradictions PERSIST even in a communist state and must be
          fought PERPETUALLY
          >> this CONTRADICTS step 4's finality -- and saying so is the
             cheapest high-value observation available in this lesson
```

### 1. Necessity

Because change accumulates slowly and synthesises suddenly, **any significant social change is always the product of a revolution** — and the formulation to carry is that **revolution is the indispensable midwife of social change**. The reason reform will not do is not impatience but power: the *Class Struggle* box supplies the antagonism generated by ownership, the *Ideology* box supplies the legitimation that makes consent available to the rulers, and the dominant class never parts with power until forced. Hence **social revolution is the necessary lever of social change**. The precedent is the **French Revolution (1789)**, the capitalist order's own founding overthrow — ⚠️ an argument from consistency, and a strong one.

### 2. Finality, which is the distinctive claim

Every earlier revolution replaced one minority with another, so exploitation was **transferred** rather than ended. The proletarian revolution is a **revolution of the majority against the minority**, after which no class remains to be exploited; it is therefore **the final** revolution. Socialism then blossoms into communism **without a further revolution**, with **to each according to his work** giving way to **to each according to his need**. The *Manifesto*'s closing declaration supplies the call to action, and the **Lenin epigraph** states the end-point: **While the State exists, there can be no freedom. When there is freedom there will be no State.**

### 3. The dissent from inside

**Mao's doctrine of permanent revolution** holds that contradictions **persist even in a communist state** and must be fought perpetually. 🔑 Note the tension openly: permanent revolution contradicts finality. An answer that names this is doing something better than criticising the theory from outside — it is showing that the tradition's own most committed wing abandoned the claim that made the theory distinctive. That observation costs one sentence and raises the band.

### Deployment in GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay

- **GS-I and GS-II.** On stems about whether structural change requires rupture, the midwife and lever formulations give the strongest statement of the case, and the finality claim marks where it overreaches.
- **GS-IV.** The ideology box is the conceptual basis for asking whether consent to an order counts as endorsement of it — the same question false consent raised at Lesson 4.
- **Essay.** The 1789 consistency argument is a sophisticated opening for prompts on order, legitimacy and the right to overthrow.

**Answer framework — the revolution paragraph, in four moves.** State necessity with the midwife formulation and its *reason* — power is never surrendered voluntarily — rather than as an assertion → give the ideology step, since legitimation is what makes reform look available when it is not → state the finality claim precisely, because it is the theory's distinctive content → close by naming the internal tension with permanent revolution, which supplies a qualification from inside the tradition rather than from its critics. ⚠️ Move four is the one that converts exposition into assessment at no cost in length.

Revision notes:

- **Any significant social change is always the product of a revolution**; **revolution is the indispensable midwife of social change**.
- *Class Struggle* box: antagonism generated by ownership. *Ideology* box: the dominant ideology legitimises the existing order.
- The dominant class never yields until forced → **social revolution is the necessary lever of social change**.
- The **French Revolution (1789)** is the capitalist order's own founding overthrow — an argument from consistency.
- Earlier revolutions: **minority replaces minority** → exploitation **transferred**.
- Proletarian revolution: **majority against minority** → therefore **the final revolution**.
- Socialism → communism **without a further revolution**; **to each according to his work** → **to each according to his need**.
- **Lenin:** **While the State exists, there can be no freedom. When there is freedom there will be no State.**
- **Mao's permanent revolution:** contradictions persist in a communist state and must be fought perpetually.
- 🔑 Permanent revolution **contradicts** the finality claim — the cheapest high-value observation in this lesson.
- ⚠️ The finality claim is Popper's target at Lesson 14 and MacIver's target at Lesson 7.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does this theory treat revolution as necessary rather than merely possible, and what makes the proletarian revolution different from every earlier one?

**Model answer:** Necessity follows from two premises, one about pace and one about power. On pace, change accumulates gradually and synthesises suddenly, so **any significant social change is always the product of a revolution** — **revolution is the indispensable midwife of social change**. On power, the *Class Struggle* box supplies antagonism generated by ownership and the *Ideology* box supplies the legitimation through which the dominant order secures consent, so the dominant class never parts with power until forced: **social revolution is the necessary lever of social change**. The precedent is given as the **French Revolution (1789)**, the capitalist order's own founding overthrow, which makes the argument one from consistency rather than from preference. ⚠️ The distinctive claim is the arithmetic. Every earlier revolution replaced one minority with another, so exploitation was transferred rather than ended; the proletarian revolution is **a revolution of the majority against the minority**, after which no class remains available to be exploited, which makes it **the final** revolution — and socialism then blossoms into communism **without a further revolution**, as **to each according to his work** gives way to **to each according to his need**. **Lenin** states the terminus: **While the State exists, there can be no freedom. When there is freedom there will be no State.** 🔑 The internal tension should be named: **Mao's permanent revolution** holds that contradictions persist even in a communist state and must be fought perpetually, which contradicts finality from inside the tradition rather than from outside it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the finality claim as rhetorical enthusiasm. It is a structural argument from the arithmetic of class — majority against minority rather than minority against minority — and it is the claim on which the theory's distinctiveness rests. Dismissing it as sloganeering means missing both Popper's objection, which attacks its unfalsifiable form, and Mao's, which abandons it while keeping the rest of the theory.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "The proletarian revolution is claimed to be the last revolution. Assess that claim." *(Assess — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (within 150 words):** The claim rests on arithmetic, and it is assessed by testing the arithmetic. **The argument.** Every earlier revolution replaced one minority with another, so exploitation was **transferred**; a **revolution of the majority against the minority** leaves no class available for exploitation, so the series ends, and socialism passes into communism **without a further revolution** as **to each according to his work** becomes **to each according to his need**. **The difficulties.** First, the claim is internal to the theory and specifies no condition under which it would be false. Second, the tradition's own wing abandons it: **Mao's permanent revolution** holds that contradictions persist even in a communist state and must be fought perpetually. Third, the historical appraisal finds a pyramid of power rebuilt **perhaps more rigidly than** under the preceding regime. ⚠️ Verdict: structurally coherent, empirically unsupported, and disowned from within.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks: 3 + 3 + 2 + 2):** 3 for reconstructing the arithmetic correctly — transfer against termination — since an answer that summarises the revolution without the majority/minority structure has not stated the claim being assessed; 3 for at least two distinct difficulties drawn from different sources, with the internal dissent from permanent revolution counting double in value because it is the hardest to anticipate; 2 for the distribution rules quoted in the right order; 2 for a three-part verdict separating coherence, evidence and internal support. Deduct 2 for treating finality as rhetoric rather than as a structural claim, and 2 for importing Popper's full case, which belongs to the next lesson.

## Lesson 14 — Popper's answer: no laws of history, only piecemeal engineering

Progress: 14 / 23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Historicism as pseudo-science, the three false prophets, the two grounds of 1957, methodological individualism, the reframed question, piecemeal against Utopian engineering, and the two-sided appraisal closing on Sorel and Kafka

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Gauba, Ch.24, PDF pp.550–554 (printed 531–535) across the four printed sub-heads, including the critical appraisal with Sorel and Kafka; Basic owner §§4, §9; Advanced owner §§6–7.
CA search: Covered by the single bounded topic-level pass of 3 October 2026; ❌ not repeated per lesson.
CA found: None usable for this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

This is the standard counter-case in every reform-versus-revolution answer, and it is also where the chapter delivers its most balanced paragraph — one that refuses to let the reform side win by default.

```text
  THE REFUTATION, AND THEN THE REFUTATION OF THE       PDF 550-554
  REFUTATION

  A. HISTORICISM, AND WHY IT IS A PSEUDO-SCIENCE
     HISTORICISM = the belief that history is governed by overarching
     LAWS that determine its course -- HEGEL, MARX, and SPENGLER
     the charge: it is 'A MASK FOR UNCRITICAL ATTACHMENT TO A
     TOTALITARIAN IDEOLOGY'
     and it is a 'PSEUDO-SCIENCE' because it is INSULATED FROM
     FALSIFICATION -- every outcome can be accommodated, so no outcome
     can refute it
     >> NOTE THE FORM OF THE ARGUMENT: the objection is NOT that the
        prediction is wrong. It is that nothing could show it wrong.

  B. THE THREE 'FALSE PROPHETS' (The Open Society and Its Enemies, 1945)
     PLATO ... the 'NOBLE LIE' / 'ROYAL FABLE' of gold, silver and iron
     HEGEL ... the state as 'THE MARCH OF GOD ON EARTH'
     MARX .... the myth of CLASS WAR
     all three: advocates of a CLOSED SOCIETY

  C. THE POVERTY OF HISTORICISM (1957) -- TWO GROUNDS
     (1) the GROWTH OF KNOWLEDGE influences history, and knowledge
         cannot be predicted -- because to predict a future discovery
         is already to possess it
     (2) social science yields laws only for FRAGMENTED AND ISOLATED
         units, not for whole societies across time
     APPLICATION: Marx's law of the CONCENTRATION OF CAPITAL is at
     best A TENDENCY, NOT A LAW

  D. METHOD AND THE REFRAMED QUESTION
     METHODOLOGICAL HOLISM (explain parts by the whole)
        vs METHODOLOGICAL INDIVIDUALISM (explain outcomes by the
        actions and situations of individuals)  <- Popper's choice
     consequence: central planning and HOLISTIC SOCIAL ENGINEERING
     are criticised as unmanageable AND unaccountable
     THE REFRAME: the question is not WHO SHOULD RULE but
        HOW DO WE DEVISE INSTITUTIONS THAT MINIMISE THE RISK OF BAD
        RULERS AND ALLOW THEIR REMOVAL WITHOUT BLOODSHED?

  E. PIECEMEAL vs UTOPIAN SOCIAL ENGINEERING -- three printed arguments
     (1) there is NO AGREEMENT on a remote, complex ideal -> imposing
         one requires AUTHORITARIAN MEANS and VIOLENCE
     (2) GENERATIONAL INJUSTICE: the present generation bears the cost
         for a benefit promised to a later one
     (3) SCIENCE ADVANCES BY STEP-BY-STEP APPROXIMATION, and social
         reform should follow the same error-correcting method
     the printed nuance: Popper 'is NOT SO AVERSE TO REVOLUTION IN THE
     REALM OF THOUGHT, AS NO BLOOD IS SPILT IN INTELLECTUAL
     REVOLUTIONS'

  F. A CRITICAL APPRAISAL -- the chapter refuses to let reform win
     SOREL (1847-1922): 'GRADUAL REFORM NEVER REALLY ACHIEVES ANYTHING
        IN THE LONG RUN' -- small concessions mitigate discontent for a
        time, but are no durable solution
     THE TWO-SIDED CAUTION:
        revolutionaries must ensure the liquidated privileged class is
           not replaced by A NEW PRIVILEGED CLASS IN A NEW GARB
        reformers must ensure the effect DOES NOT EVAPORATE, and must
           promote EQUALITY OF OPPORTUNITY and REDUCED DISPARITIES of
           power, prestige and wealth
     KAFKA: 'EVERY REVOLUTION EVAPORATES AND ONLY LEAVES BEHIND THE
        SLIME OF A NEW BUREAUCRACY'
```

### 1. The argument's form, which is what candidates misreport

Popper's objection to historicism is **not** that its predictions are mistaken. It is that the theory is **insulated from falsification** — any outcome can be absorbed, so no outcome can refute it — which makes it a **pseudo-science** and, in the strong formulation, **a mask for uncritical attachment to a totalitarian ideology**. 🔑 Getting the form right is worth more than any amount of detail, because an answer that says "Popper proved Marx wrong about history" has attributed to him an empirical claim he explicitly declines to make.

### 2. The targets and the two grounds

*The Open Society and Its Enemies* (1945) names three **false prophets** of a closed society: **Plato**'s **noble lie** or **royal fable** of gold, silver and iron; **Hegel**'s state as **the march of God on earth**; and **Marx**'s myth of class war. **Spengler** is named alongside Hegel and Marx as a historicist.

*The Poverty of Historicism* (1957) gives **two grounds**. First, the **growth of knowledge** influences the course of history and cannot itself be predicted, since predicting a future discovery would be to possess it already. Second, social science can establish laws only for **fragmented and isolated units**, not for whole societies across time. ⚠️ Applied, this downgrades Marx's law of the concentration of capital to **a tendency, not a law** — a precise, limited and therefore powerful conclusion.

### 3. Method, the reframe and the two engineerings

Popper prefers **methodological individualism** to **methodological holism**, explaining outcomes by the actions and situations of individuals rather than by properties of wholes; central planning and **holistic social engineering** are criticised accordingly. The reframe follows: the political question is not **who should rule** but **how to devise institutions that minimise the risk of bad rulers** and permit their removal without bloodshed. 🔑 That reframe is the single most reusable sentence in Stage Two for GS-II, because it converts any "who is best suited to decide" stem into an institutional-design question.

**Piecemeal** against **Utopian** social engineering rests on three printed arguments: there is **no agreement** on a remote and complex ideal, so imposing one requires **authoritarian means and violence**; the **generational-injustice** argument, that the present generation bears costs for a benefit promised to a later one; and the **approximation** argument, that science advances step by step and social reform should use the same error-correcting method. 📝 The printed nuance is worth a clause: Popper **is not so averse to revolution in the realm of thought, as no blood is spilt in intellectual revolutions**.

### 4. The appraisal, which is the paragraph that wins marks

The chapter refuses to let gradualism win by default. **Sorel (1847–1922)** holds that **gradual reform never really achieves anything in the long run**: concessions mitigate discontent for a time but supply no durable solution. Then comes the two-sided caution: revolutionaries must ensure the liquidated privileged class is not replaced by **a new privileged class in a new garb**; reformers must ensure the effect does not evaporate, and must actively promote **equality of opportunity** and **reduced disparities of power, prestige and wealth**. The closing line is **Kafka**: **Every revolution evaporates and only leaves behind the slime of a new bureaucracy.**

🔑 Pair each quotation with its proposition. Sorel proves *the reform side's weakest point*; Kafka proves *the revolution side's weakest point*; and together they are the balanced-verdict paragraph for every reform-versus-revolution answer in the subject.

### Deployment in GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay

- **GS-II.** The reframed question is the strongest available opening for stems on institutional design, checks on power, or incremental against comprehensive reform.
- **GS-IV.** The generational-injustice argument is a clean statement of why present costs imposed for promised future benefits require independent justification.
- **Essay.** The Sorel–Kafka pair gives an essay on change a genuine dilemma rather than a preference, and the two-sided caution supplies its conclusion.

**Answer framework — the reform-versus-revolution verdict, in four moves.** State the falsifiability objection in its correct form, not as a claim that the prediction failed → give one of the two grounds, preferably the knowledge-growth ground, because it is self-contained → set piecemeal against Utopian engineering with one of the three arguments → close on the two-sided caution with Sorel on one side and Kafka on the other, so the conclusion is a condition rather than a side. ⚠️ Answers that end by choosing reform are weaker than answers that end by stating what each side must guarantee.

Revision notes:

- **Historicism** = history governed by overarching laws — **Hegel, Marx, Spengler**; charged as **a mask for uncritical attachment to a totalitarian ideology**.
- 🔑 It is a **pseudo-science** because **insulated from falsification** — the objection is to the form, not to the content.
- *The Open Society and Its Enemies* (**1945**): three **false prophets** — Plato's **noble lie**/**royal fable**, Hegel's **march of God on earth**, Marx's class-war myth; all advocate a **closed society**.
- *The Poverty of Historicism* (**1957**), two grounds: knowledge grows, influences history and **cannot be predicted**; social science yields laws only for **fragmented and isolated units**.
- Marx's concentration of capital is **a tendency, not a law**.
- **Methodological holism** against **methodological individualism**; holistic social engineering criticised.
- 🔑 The reframe: not **who should rule** but **how to devise institutions that minimise the risk of bad rulers**.
- **Piecemeal against Utopian engineering**, three arguments: no agreement on a remote ideal → **authoritarian means and violence**; **generational injustice**; **step-by-step approximation**.
- 📝 Popper is **not so averse to revolution in the realm of thought, as no blood is spilt in intellectual revolutions**.
- **Sorel:** **gradual reform never really achieves anything in the long run**.
- Two-sided caution: no **new privileged class in a new garb**; reform must not **evaporate**, and must promote **equality of opportunity** and reduced disparities of power, prestige and wealth.
- **Kafka:** **Every revolution evaporates and only leaves behind the slime of a new bureaucracy.**

### Concept check

**Question:** State Popper's objection to historicism in its correct form, and explain why the chapter's appraisal does not let gradualism win the argument.

**Model answer:** The objection is methodological, not predictive. **Historicism** — the belief that history is governed by overarching laws, attributed to **Hegel**, **Marx** and **Spengler** — is called **a mask for uncritical attachment to a totalitarian ideology** and a **pseudo-science**, because it is **insulated from falsification**: any outcome can be accommodated, so no outcome can refute it. ⚠️ The charge is therefore about the **form** of the theory; it is not a claim that a particular prediction failed. *The Open Society and Its Enemies* (1945) names three **false prophets** of a closed society — Plato's **noble lie** of gold, silver and iron, Hegel's state as **the march of God on earth**, and Marx's myth of class war. *The Poverty of Historicism* (1957) adds two grounds: the **growth of knowledge** shapes history and cannot be predicted, since predicting a discovery is possessing it; and social science yields laws only for **fragmented and isolated units**. Applied, Marx's concentration of capital is **a tendency, not a law**. Method follows: **methodological individualism** rather than holism, so holistic engineering and central planning are criticised, and the political question is reframed — not **who should rule** but **how to devise institutions that minimise the risk of bad rulers**. **Piecemeal** engineering is preferred to **Utopian** on three grounds: disagreement about a remote ideal invites **authoritarian means and violence**; the present generation bears costs for a later one's benefit; and reform should proceed by **step-by-step approximation**. 🔑 The appraisal then refuses to award the argument. **Sorel** holds that **gradual reform never really achieves anything in the long run**, and the caution runs both ways — revolutionaries must prevent **a new privileged class in a new garb**, reformers must prevent the effect from evaporating and must promote **equality of opportunity** and reduced disparities of power, prestige and wealth — closing with **Kafka**: **Every revolution evaporates and only leaves behind the slime of a new bureaucracy.**

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not write that Popper disproved Marx's predictions. He argues that a theory capable of absorbing every outcome makes no testable prediction at all, which is a different and stronger charge; and he explicitly allows revolution **in the realm of thought**, since no blood is spilt there. Presenting him as a conservative opponent of all radical change misstates both the argument and its scope.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Piecemeal reform is safer than revolution, but safety is not the same as sufficiency." Discuss. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (within 150 words):** **Safety, established.** Utopian engineering requires agreement on a remote and complex ideal that does not exist, so imposing it demands **authoritarian means and violence**; it charges the present generation for a later one's benefit; and it abandons the **step-by-step approximation** by which error is corrected. Piecemeal reform preserves reversibility, which is why the question is reframed as how to **devise institutions that minimise the risk of bad rulers** rather than who should rule. **Sufficiency, unestablished.** **Sorel** replies that **gradual reform never really achieves anything in the long run**, since concessions mitigate discontent without removing its source; the printed caution accordingly requires reformers to prevent the effect from **evaporating** and to promote **equality of opportunity** and reduced disparities of power, prestige and wealth. ⚠️ And the revolutionary route faces **Kafka's** verdict. Discussion's conclusion: neither route is self-justifying — each is defensible only with its own guarantee attached.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks: 3 + 3 + 2 + 2):** 3 for establishing safety with at least two of the three printed arguments, since one argument plus assertion reads as preference rather than discussion; 3 for the insufficiency case led by Sorel's proposition and the reformers' half of the two-sided caution — an answer that merely says "reform is slow" has not engaged; 2 for the reframed question used as the institutional payoff of reversibility; 2 for a conclusion stating the guarantee each route must supply instead of choosing a side. Deduct 2 for writing that Popper refuted Marx's predictions rather than his method, and 2 for omitting the appraisal entirely, which is where the chapter's own balance lives.

## Lesson 15 — What development means, and who the "third world" was

Progress: 15 / 23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The purpose for which the concept was built, the empirical/normative split restated as a method, the three printed definitions, and the third-world category with its printed caveat

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Gauba, Ch.25, PDF pp.555–558 (printed 536–539) for the purpose clause, the three definitions and the third-world block; Basic owner §§3, §16; Advanced owner §§4, §8C.
CA search: Covered by the single bounded topic-level pass of 3 October 2026; ❌ not repeated per lesson.
CA found: None usable, and ❌ none permitted: no country is described as developed or underdeveloped anywhere in this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Stage Three opens by fixing a conception. A verified fifteen-mark question demands that the conception of development be settled before anything is claimed about it, and two further questions turn on the same fix. ⚠️ Every country figure in this lesson is book-period and is written in the past tense.

```text
  THE CONCEPT, ITS METHOD, AND ITS SUBJECT             PDF 555-558

  WHY THE CONCEPT WAS BUILT
     for the NEW NATIONS that became independent after 1939-45
     -> development is not a neutral description; it was devised as a
        GOAL-RELATIVE concept for a specific historical task

  WHAT IT MEANS
     transformation into a STRONGER, MORE ORGANIZED, MORE EFFICIENT and
     MORE EFFECTIVE form -- 'MORE SATISFYING IN TERMS OF HUMAN WANTS
     AND ASPIRATIONS'
     measurable on an EMPIRICAL SCALE
        ... while PROGRESS applies a NORMATIVE criterion  [Lesson 10]

  THE METHOD (and the trap inside it)
     step 1: identify the characteristics of an ADVANCED society
     step 2: measure the DISTANCE of a given society from that goal
     >> THE TRAP: step 1 smuggles in a model. Whoever fixes the
        'advanced society' has already decided what development is.
        This is exactly what Frank attacks at Lesson 17.

  THREE PRINTED DEFINITIONS
     MITTELMAN (Out from Underdevelopment, 1988)
        development = 'the INCREASING CAPACITY TO MAKE RATIONAL USE OF
        NATURAL AND HUMAN RESOURCES FOR SOCIAL ENDS'
        underdevelopment = 'THE BLOCKAGE WHICH FORESTALLS A RATIONAL
        TRANSFORMATION OF THE SOCIAL STRUCTURE'
        >> note: UNDERDEVELOPMENT is a BLOCKAGE, not a backlog
     BARAN (The Political Economy of Growth, 1957)
        'a FAR-REACHING TRANSFORMATION of society's ECONOMIC, SOCIAL
        and POLITICAL structure' which 'HAS NEVER BEEN A SMOOTH,
        HARMONIOUS PROCESS'
     RODNEY (1974)
        'A MANY-SIDED PROCESS' -- skill and capacity, greater freedom,
        creativity, SELF-DISCIPLINE, RESPONSIBILITY and material
        well-being
     >> THREE DEFINITIONS, THREE EMPHASES: CAPACITY (Mittelman),
        STRUCTURE (Baran), PERSON (Rodney). An answer that names all
        three has already refused the GDP reduction.

  THE 'THIRD WORLD'
     coined by ALFRED SAUVY in a 1952 article
     THE THIRD-ESTATE PARALLEL: numerous | excluded from power |
        carrying REVOLUTIONARY POTENTIAL
     three printed characteristics; colonial-drain indicators;
        a WESTERN-EDUCATED ELITE; recurrent MILITARY RULE
     NON-ALIGNMENT -- INDIA named as a leading member
     THE PRINTED CAVEAT: after 1991 the term 'SHOULD HAVE BECOME
        OUTDATED ... YET IT STILL MAINTAINS ITS IDENTITY DUE TO LONG
        USAGE'
```

### 1. The purpose clause and the method

The concept was evolved for the **new nations that became independent after 1939–45**, which is itself an analytical fact: development is **goal-relative**, built for a task, and not a neutral description of social movement. Its content is transformation into a **stronger, more organized, more efficient and more effective** form — and, decisively, **more satisfying in terms of human wants and aspirations** — measurable on an **empirical scale**, while **progress** applies a **normative** criterion.

The method is two-step: identify the characteristics of an **advanced society**, then measure the distance of a given society from that goal. 🔑 The trap is in step one. Whoever fixes the description of the advanced society has already decided what development is, which is exactly the objection Frank raises at Lesson 17. Naming the trap at the definition stage, rather than waiting for the criticism, is what distinguishes a graded answer.

### 2. The three definitions, each with a different emphasis

**Mittelman (*Out from Underdevelopment*, 1988):** development is **the increasing capacity to make rational use of natural and human resources for social ends**, and underdevelopment is **the blockage which forestalls a rational transformation of the social structure**. 🔑 The word **blockage** matters: underdevelopment is a condition produced by an obstruction, not a backlog waiting to be cleared by time — which is the premise the dependency argument will build on.

**Baran (*The Political Economy of Growth*, 1957):** development is **a far-reaching transformation of society's economic, social and political structure**, and it **has never been a smooth, harmonious process**. ⚠️ The second clause blocks any account in which development is a managed glide path.

**Rodney (1974):** development is **a many-sided process** implying increased skill and capacity, greater freedom, creativity, **self-discipline**, responsibility and material well-being — a person-level definition, not an aggregate one.

⚠️ Named together, the three refuse the GDP reduction before any critique is mounted: one emphasises **capacity**, one **structure**, one the **person**, and none of them is a measure of output.

### 3. The third world

The term was coined by **Alfred Sauvy** in a **1952** article, on the **third-estate parallel** — a group that is numerous, excluded from power, and carries revolutionary potential. Three printed characteristics follow, along with the colonial-drain indicators, the presence of a **Western-educated elite** and the pattern of recurrent military rule. **Non-alignment** is named as the category's political expression, with **India named as a leading member**. ✅ This is one of the four Indian anchors the source itself supplies.

The printed caveat is the most quotable line in the block: with the second world dissolved after 1991, the term **should have become outdated** — **yet it still maintains its identity due to long usage**. ⚠️ All associated figures are book-period and are attributed as reported in a 2009 text; ❌ none is presented as current.

### Deployment in GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay

- **GS-II.** Any stem containing "development" is opened by fixing the conception, because the three definitions make the subsequent argument possible; the blockage formulation is the bridge into causal questions about why gaps persist.
- **GS-IV.** Rodney's person-level definition, with **self-discipline and responsibility** inside it, is the cleanest statement that development has a character dimension and not only a material one.
- **Essay.** The purpose clause plus the step-one trap gives an essay a non-obvious thesis: the concept of development already contains a judgment about what a society should look like.

**Answer framework — the conception-first opening, in four moves.** Name the purpose for which the concept exists, which immediately denies its neutrality → fix the content on the empirical scale and contrast it with normative progress in one clause → cite one of the three definitions chosen for the stem, capacity for a capability stem, blockage for a causal stem, person-level for an ethics stem → expose the step-one trap, so the answer's later criticisms are already licensed. ⚠️ Move three is where most answers waste the opportunity by giving a generic definition instead of selecting the one that matches the demand.

Revision notes:

- The concept was evolved for nations independent after **1939–45** — therefore **goal-relative**, not neutral.
- Content: **stronger, more organized, more efficient, more effective**, and **more satisfying in terms of human wants and aspirations**.
- **Empirical** scale for development; **normative** criterion for **progress**.
- Method: identify the **advanced society**, then measure distance. 🔑 Step one smuggles in a model.
- **Mittelman (1988):** **increasing capacity to make rational use of natural and human resources for social ends**; underdevelopment is **the blockage which forestalls a rational transformation of the social structure**.
- **Baran (1957):** **a far-reaching transformation** of economic, social and political structure, which **has never been a smooth, harmonious process**.
- **Rodney (1974):** **a many-sided process** — skill, freedom, creativity, **self-discipline**, responsibility, material well-being.
- 🔑 Three emphases: **capacity · structure · person** — together they refuse the GDP reduction.
- **Third world:** coined by **Sauvy (1952)** on the **third-estate parallel** — numerous, excluded, revolutionary potential.
- Markers: colonial-drain indicators, a **Western-educated elite**, recurrent military rule, **non-alignment with India a leading member**.
- Printed caveat: after 1991 it **should have become outdated** — **yet it still maintains its identity due to long usage**.
- ❌ No country is classified as developed or underdeveloped; all figures are book-period.

### Concept check

**Question:** Fix the conception of development as this chapter fixes it, and explain why the method it recommends contains its own vulnerability.

**Model answer:** The concept was evolved for the **new nations that became independent after 1939–45**, so it is goal-relative rather than neutral. Its content is transformation into a **stronger, more organized, more efficient and more effective** form — **more satisfying in terms of human wants and aspirations** — assessed on an **empirical scale**, while **progress** applies a **normative** criterion. Three printed definitions give it substance from different directions. **Mittelman (1988)** defines development as **the increasing capacity to make rational use of natural and human resources for social ends** and underdevelopment as **the blockage which forestalls a rational transformation of the social structure**. **Baran (1957)** defines it as **a far-reaching transformation of society's economic, social and political structure** which **has never been a smooth, harmonious process**. **Rodney (1974)** defines it as **a many-sided process** implying skill and capacity, greater freedom, creativity, **self-discipline**, responsibility and material well-being. ⚠️ Together they emphasise capacity, structure and the person, which is why none of them reduces to output. The recommended method is to identify the characteristics of an **advanced society** and then measure a given society's distance from that goal. 🔑 The vulnerability is in the first step: whoever specifies the advanced society has already determined what development is, so the measurement merely records distance from a model chosen in advance. That is the opening for the objection that stage theories assume a single path and ignore radically different starting points, and it is also why the chapter needs four rival models rather than one scale.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the third-world category as a neutral or current classification. It was coined by **Sauvy (1952)** on a political analogy with the third estate, and the chapter itself records that after 1991 the term **should have become outdated** and survives **due to long usage**. Using it as a present-tense description of any country both misreports the source and breaches this edition's prohibition on classifying present-day entities.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Underdevelopment is a blockage, not a backlog." Comment. *(Comment — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (within 150 words):** The proposition compresses **Mittelman's (1988)** pair of definitions and the difference it makes is causal. **Backlog reading.** If underdevelopment were a backlog, it would be an earlier position on one path, and the remedy would be acceleration along that path — which is what the two-step method invites, since it identifies an **advanced society** and measures distance from it. **Blockage reading.** Mittelman instead defines underdevelopment as **the blockage which forestalls a rational transformation of the social structure**, so something is actively preventing the transformation; **Baran (1957)** adds that development **has never been a smooth, harmonious process**. The remedy then becomes removal of the obstruction, not speed. ⚠️ The readings also differ in blame-neutrality: a backlog implicates no one, a blockage requires naming what blocks. Verdict: the blockage reading is the chapter's, and it is what makes a causal account of persistent underdevelopment possible at all.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks: 3 + 3 + 2 + 2):** 3 for stating both readings as rival causal claims rather than as definitions, since a *comment* directive rewards the consequence; 3 for Mittelman's pair reproduced accurately, with **blockage** doing the work and the contrast with the two-step method made explicit; 2 for a second definition — Baran's non-smoothness or Rodney's many-sidedness — used to reinforce rather than to pad; 2 for a verdict that explains what the blockage reading makes possible, namely a causal explanation. Deduct 2 for naming any present-day country as underdeveloped, and 2 for answering with growth indicators, which this topic does not supply.

## Lesson 16 — Why underdevelopment persists: the liberal account and the dependency reply

Progress: 16 / 23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The liberal stages account with its named exponents, the Marxist chain from Lenin to Amin, the centre–periphery model with delinking, unequal exchange, the primary-product trap, and the printed conclusion on strategy

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Gauba, Ch.25, PDF pp.558–561 (printed 539–542) for the LIBERAL VIEW, the MARXIST VIEW, the *Unequal Exchange* box and the printed CONCLUSION; Basic owner §§14, §16; Advanced owner §§6, §8C.
CA search: Covered by the single bounded topic-level pass of 3 October 2026; ❌ not repeated per lesson.
CA found: None usable. ❌ No current trade, aid or investment data is asserted; that content belongs to `International-Relations/` and the current-affairs folders.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Lesson 15 fixed the conception. This lesson supplies the two rival **explanations** of why the gap persists, and they differ in a way worth stating at the outset: the liberal account treats underdevelopment as an **initial condition** and the Marxist account treats it as a **produced relation**. Everything else follows from that.

```text
  TWO EXPLANATIONS OF THE SAME GAP                     PDF 558-561

  LIBERAL VIEW -- underdevelopment is an EARLIER STAGE
     the path: traditional/premodern  ->  modern industrial
     a modern system does more than tax, order and defence -- it
        IMPROVES THE QUALITY OF LIFE
     people COMMUNICATE DEMANDS; government relies on LEGITIMACY
     three markers of the transition:
        DIFFERENTIATION of roles and institutions
        loyalty moves from the NARROW GROUP to the NATION
        status and role move from ASCRIBED to ACHIEVED
     exponents: BRYCE (Modern Democracies, 1921) | CARL FRIEDRICH
        (1937) | ALMOND and COLEMAN (1960) | ALMOND and POWELL (1966)
        | PYE (Aspects of Political Development, 1966)
     policy implication: stay OPEN to FREE TRADE, rely on the MARKET

  MARXIST VIEW -- underdevelopment is a PRODUCED RELATION
     LENIN, Imperialism: The Highest Stage of Capitalism (1916)
        underdeveloped countries = the PROLETARIAT of the international
        struggle
     BARAN (1957)
        today's advanced countries accumulated by EXPLOITING COLONIES
        -- a route present-day developing countries CANNOT REPLICATE
     FRANK, Capitalism and Underdevelopment in Latin America (1967)
        CENTRE-PERIPHERY / METROPOLIS-SATELLITE:
        the development OF the centre PRODUCES the underdevelopment
        OF the periphery -- the same process, two ends
        the relation SURVIVES POLITICAL INDEPENDENCE
        -> prescription: DELINKING
     RODNEY (Africa) and CUMINGS (Asia) confirm the pattern
     AMIN, Accumulation on a World Scale (1974)
     UNEQUAL EXCHANGE box -- the terms on which periphery labour is
        traded against centre labour
     >> the DEPARTURE this marks: orthodox Marxism explains change by
        FORCES vs RELATIONS of production WITHIN a society; dependency
        theory explains it by a relation BETWEEN societies
     THE PRINCIPAL NEO-MARXIST TENET
        underdevelopment is NOT A STAGE ON THE ROAD TO CAPITALISM
        but a CONDITION OF DOMINATION
     the PRIMARY-PRODUCT TRAP: exporting raw materials locks in the
        relation -> the CARTEL prescription, with OPEC (1960) as the
        printed instance

  THE PRINTED CONCLUSION -- the author's own strategy
     SOUTH-SOUTH COOPERATION
     BORROWED **plus** INDIGENOUS technology, because Western
        technology suits 'an AFFLUENT, HIGHLY MOTIVATED and SMALL
        POPULATION'
     a MODICUM of competitive spirit, without CUT-THROAT competition
     welfare designed to BUILD HUMAN RESOURCES, 'lest they generate
        VESTED INTERESTS OF THE INCOMPETENT AND INDOLENT'
     no 'LUXURY OF CONSUMERISM'
     developing countries as a possible 'BLEND OF MATERIAL AND
        SPIRITUAL VALUES'
```

### 1. The liberal account

Underdevelopment is read as an **earlier stage** on a single path from traditional to modern industrial society. A modern system is distinguished by doing more than levying taxes, keeping order and providing defence: it **improves the quality of life**, people **communicate demands** to it, and it relies on **legitimacy** rather than on force. Three markers identify the transition: **differentiation** of roles and institutions, the movement of loyalty from the narrow group to the nation, and the shift from **ascribed** to **achieved** status and role. ⚠️ All three are the same direction Lesson 10's six transition pairs described, which is why the liberal account feels natural — and why its naturalness is the thing under attack.

The named exponents are **Bryce (*Modern Democracies*, 1921)**, **Carl Friedrich (1937)**, **Almond and Coleman (1960)**, **Almond and Powell (1966)** and **Pye (*Aspects of Political Development*, 1966)**. 📝 Bryce appears here in his political-development function; ➡️ his account of democracy belongs to **Topic 22**. The policy implication printed is that developing economies should remain open to **free trade** and rely on the **market**.

### 2. The Marxist account and the dependency reply

**Lenin (*Imperialism: The Highest Stage of Capitalism*, 1916)** casts underdeveloped countries as the **proletariat of the international struggle**. **Baran (1957)** adds the decisive asymmetry: today's advanced capitalist countries accumulated by exploiting colonies, which present-day developing countries **cannot replicate**, since there are no colonies available to them.

**Frank (*Capitalism and Underdevelopment in Latin America*, 1967)** supplies the model: **centre–periphery**, or **metropolis–satellite**, in which the development **of** the centre **produces** the underdevelopment **of** the periphery. 🔑 The claim is that these are one process seen from two ends, not two independent conditions — which is why the relation **survives political independence** and why the prescription is **delinking**. **Rodney** confirms it for Africa and **Cumings** for Asia; **Amin (*Accumulation on a World Scale*, 1974)** generalises it; and the **Unequal Exchange** box supplies the terms on which peripheral labour trades against central labour.

⚠️ Name the departure this marks, because it is the depth point: orthodox Marxism explains change by the contradiction between forces and relations of production **within** a society, while dependency theory explains it by a relation **between** societies. The principal neo-Marxist tenet follows — underdevelopment is **not a stage on the road to capitalism but a condition of domination**. The **primary-product trap** is its mechanism, and the printed remedy is a **cartel**, with **OPEC (1960)** named as the instance. ➡️ OPEC's institutional history belongs to `International-Relations/`.

### 3. The printed conclusion

The chapter's own strategy is neither delinking nor open markets, and it is worth reproducing because it is the author speaking: **south–south cooperation**; **borrowed plus indigenous technology**, because Western technology suits **an affluent, highly motivated and small population**; a **modicum** of competitive spirit without importing **cut-throat competition**; welfare designed to **build human resources**, **lest they generate vested interests of the incompetent and indolent**; no **luxury of consumerism**; and the closing proposition that developing countries can exemplify a **blend of material and spiritual values**. ⚠️ The welfare clause is a conditional defence of welfare, not a rejection of it, and should be reproduced with its purpose attached.

### Deployment in GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay

- **GS-II.** For stems on persistent gaps, technology choice or the limits of market-led catch-up, the centre–periphery model supplies the causal hypothesis and the printed conclusion supplies a middle path that is neither autarkic nor laissez-faire. ⚠️ Trade regimes and current data belong elsewhere.
- **GS-IV.** The welfare clause — build capacity rather than create dependence — is a precise statement of a standing dilemma in public provision, without any present-day programme named.
- **Essay.** The one-process-two-ends formulation is a strong thesis for prompts on inequality between nations, provided the liberal account is stated at full strength first.

**Answer framework — the causal explanation, in four moves.** State both explanations as rivals about the **kind** of fact underdevelopment is — initial condition against produced relation → give the liberal markers, because an answer that only attacks the liberal view has not stated it → run the dependency chain with one named work and the centre–periphery formulation → close on the printed conclusion, which supplies a qualified position rather than a choice. ⚠️ Answers that end at delinking are weaker than answers that end at borrowed-plus-indigenous technology, because the latter engages the obvious objection that delinking forfeits the gains of exchange.

Revision notes:

- Liberal view: underdevelopment as an **earlier stage** on one path; modern systems **improve the quality of life** and rely on **legitimacy**.
- Three markers: **differentiation**; loyalty from narrow group to **nation**; **ascribed → achieved** status and role.
- Exponents: **Bryce (1921), Carl Friedrich (1937), Almond and Coleman (1960), Almond and Powell (1966), Pye (1966)**; implication — **open trade, market reliance**.
- **Lenin (1916):** underdeveloped countries as the **proletariat of the international struggle**.
- **Baran (1957):** advanced countries accumulated through colonies — a route that **cannot be replicated**.
- 🔑 **Frank (1967): centre–periphery / metropolis–satellite** — the centre's development **produces** the periphery's underdevelopment; one process, two ends; it **survives political independence** → **delinking**.
- **Rodney** (Africa), **Cumings** (Asia), **Amin (1974)**; the **Unequal Exchange** box.
- ⚠️ The departure: orthodox Marxism explains change **within** a society; dependency explains it **between** societies.
- Principal tenet: underdevelopment is **not a stage** toward capitalism but a **condition of domination**.
- The **primary-product trap**; the **cartel** prescription with **OPEC (1960)**.
- Printed conclusion: **south–south cooperation**; **borrowed plus indigenous technology** (Western technology suits **an affluent, highly motivated and small population**); a **modicum** of competition without **cut-throat competition**; welfare to **build human resources**, **lest they generate vested interests of the incompetent and indolent**; no **luxury of consumerism**; a possible **blend of material and spiritual values**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Set the liberal and dependency explanations of underdevelopment against each other, and state the chapter's own conclusion on strategy.

**Model answer:** They disagree about what kind of fact underdevelopment is. **The liberal account treats it as an earlier stage** on a single path from traditional to modern industrial society, marked by **differentiation** of roles and institutions, the movement of loyalty from the narrow group to the nation, and the shift from **ascribed** to **achieved** status and role; a modern system does more than tax, order and defend — it **improves the quality of life**, receives communicated demands and rests on **legitimacy**. Its exponents are **Bryce (1921)**, **Carl Friedrich (1937)**, **Almond and Coleman (1960)**, **Almond and Powell (1966)** and **Pye (1966)**, and its policy implication is openness to **free trade** and reliance on the **market**. **The dependency account treats it as a produced relation.** **Lenin (1916)** makes underdeveloped countries the **proletariat of the international struggle**; **Baran (1957)** notes that the advanced countries accumulated through colonies, a route that cannot be replicated; and **Frank (1967)** supplies the **centre–periphery** or **metropolis–satellite** model in which the centre's development **produces** the periphery's underdevelopment — one process seen from two ends, surviving political independence, hence **delinking**. **Rodney**, **Cumings** and **Amin (1974)** extend it, and the **Unequal Exchange** box states its terms. ⚠️ The departure from orthodox Marxism is that the explanatory relation runs **between** societies rather than within one, and the principal tenet is that underdevelopment is **not a stage on the road to capitalism but a condition of domination**, locked in by the **primary-product trap** and answerable, on this view, by a **cartel** — **OPEC (1960)**. 🔑 The chapter's own conclusion takes neither route whole: **south–south cooperation**; **borrowed plus indigenous technology**, because Western technology suits **an affluent, highly motivated and small population**; a **modicum** of competitive spirit without **cut-throat competition**; welfare designed to **build human resources**, **lest they generate vested interests of the incompetent and indolent**; no **luxury of consumerism**; and the possibility of a **blend of material and spiritual values**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat centre–periphery as a claim that rich countries are merely richer. The claim is that one process generates both outcomes, which is why the relation is said to survive political independence and why delinking rather than catch-up is prescribed. Reading it as an inequality statistic loses the causal content and leaves the liberal stage account untouched.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "The development of one region can be the cause of another's underdevelopment." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (within 150 words):** The proposition is **Frank's (1967)** and it is examined by testing its causal structure. **The case for.** On the **centre–periphery** or **metropolis–satellite** model these are one process seen from two ends: surplus moves outward, the periphery specialises in primary products, and the relation **survives political independence** — hence **delinking**. **Baran (1957)** supplies the asymmetry that makes catch-up impossible by the original route, since advanced countries accumulated through colonies; **Amin (1974)** and the **Unequal Exchange** box give its terms. ⚠️ The theoretical departure is real: the explanation runs **between** societies, not within one, so underdevelopment becomes **a condition of domination**, not a stage. **The limits.** The liberal account identifies internal variables — **differentiation**, the move from **ascribed to achieved** status — that the model underweights, and delinking forfeits gains from exchange. Verdict: a strong causal hypothesis; a weak policy prescription, which is why the chapter prefers **borrowed plus indigenous technology**.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks: 3 + 3 + 2 + 2):** 3 for the one-process-two-ends structure stated as a causal claim, with the survival of the relation after independence used as its evidence; 3 for the limits drawn from the liberal account's internal variables and from the cost of delinking, rather than from a general preference for markets; 2 for naming the departure from orthodox Marxism, between societies rather than within one; 2 for closing on the chapter's own middle strategy instead of selecting a camp. Deduct 2 for asserting any current trade, aid or investment figure, and 2 for treating centre–periphery as an inequality statistic rather than a causal mechanism.

## Lesson 17 — Path 1: the market-society model, and why its starting point is contested

Progress: 17 / 23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Development as modernization as Westernization, Coleman's modern society, Rostow's five stages and Organski's four, and the three printed criticisms ending on the moral-strength trade-off

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Gauba, Ch.25, §IV at PDF pp.562–563 (printed 543–544) for the market-society model and its three printed criticisms; Basic owner §§8B, §16; Advanced owner §§7, §8C.
CA search: Covered by the single bounded topic-level pass of 3 October 2026; ❌ not repeated per lesson.
CA found: None usable for this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Four rival paths follow, and the first is the one the others define themselves against. Its examinable value is not the stage lists but the **three criticisms**, because each attacks a different thing: the first attacks its history, the second its social cost, the third its conception of the person.

```text
  THE MODEL, AND THREE DIFFERENT KINDS OF ATTACK      PDF 562-563

  THE EQUATION
     DEVELOPMENT = MODERNIZATION = WESTERNIZATION
        politically ... liberal-democratic structures
        economically . competitive markets
        socially ..... equality of opportunity, respect for MERIT

  COLEMAN'S QUOTED DEFINITION OF A MODERN SOCIETY
     URBANISATION | WIDESPREAD LITERACY | HIGH PER-CAPITA INCOME |
     GEOGRAPHICAL AND SOCIAL MOBILITY | COMMERCIALISATION AND
     INDUSTRIALISATION | PENETRATIVE MASS COMMUNICATION | WIDESPREAD
     PARTICIPATION

  GROWTH AS A NECESSARY CONDITION -- two stage theories
     ROSTOW (five)   1 TRADITIONAL
                     2 PRECONDITIONS FOR TAKE-OFF (transitional)
                     3 TAKE-OFF
                     4 DRIVE TO MATURITY
                     5 HIGH MASS CONSUMPTION
     ORGANSKI (four) 1 PRIMITIVE UNIFICATION
                     2 INDUSTRIALISATION
                     3 NATIONAL WELFARE
                     4 POLITICS OF ABUNDANCE

  THREE CRITICISMS -- each attacks a DIFFERENT thing
   +---+--------------+---------------------------------------------+
   | 1 | ITS HISTORY  | FRANK (1971): Western underdevelopment was   |
   |   |              | an ORIGINAL condition; developing-country    |
   |   |              | underdevelopment is a CONSEQUENCE of         |
   |   |              | European capitalist expansion.               |
   |   |              | 'HOW CAN THEY FOLLOW THE SAME PATTERN OF     |
   |   |              | DEVELOPMENT WHEN THEIR STARTING POINTS ARE   |
   |   |              | RADICALLY DIFFERENT?'                        |
   +---+--------------+---------------------------------------------+
   | 2 | ITS SOCIAL   | COMMUNITARIAN objection: societies that      |
   |   | COST         | 'COMPENSATED FOR THEIR MATERIAL POVERTY BY A |
   |   |              | MODICUM OF SOCIAL SOLIDARITY' lose that      |
   |   |              | solidarity in the transition                 |
   +---+--------------+---------------------------------------------+
   | 3 | ITS IDEA OF  | PROFIT objection: even HIGHER EDUCATION, ART |
   |   | THE PERSON   | and CULTURE become geared to MARKET NEEDS;   |
   |   |              | human beings are 'REDUCED TO SELF-SEEKING    |
   |   |              | ANIMALS'                                     |
   +---+--------------+---------------------------------------------+
   THE PRINTED TRADE-OFF: material welfare is gained 'AT THE EXPENSE
   OF THEIR MORAL STRENGTH'
```

### 1. The equation and its content

The model equates **development with modernization and modernization with Westernization**: liberal-democratic structures politically, competitive markets economically, and equality of opportunity with respect for **merit** socially. **Coleman's quoted definition of a modern society** supplies the checklist — **urbanisation, widespread literacy, high per-capita income, geographical and social mobility, commercialisation and industrialisation, penetrative mass communication and widespread participation**. ⚠️ Note that this *is* the step-one specification Lesson 15 warned about: the advanced society has now been described, and everything else becomes distance from it.

Growth is treated as a **necessary condition**, and two stage theories supply the sequence. **Rostow's five stages**: traditional, preconditions for take-off, take-off, drive to maturity, high mass consumption. **Organski's four**: primitive unification, industrialisation, national welfare, politics of abundance. 📝 Learn them as ordered lists; they are frequently examined by sequence rather than by content.

### 2. The three criticisms, kept distinct

**Criticism one attacks its history.** **Frank (1971)** argues that Western underdevelopment was an **original** condition, while developing-country underdevelopment is a **consequence** of European capitalist expansion — and puts it as a question: **How can they follow the same pattern of development when their starting points are radically different?** 🔑 Pair the quotation with the proposition it proves: **stage theories smuggle in a false analogy**. The objection is not that the stages are undesirable but that the sequence was never available.

**Criticism two attacks its social cost.** Societies that **compensated for their material poverty by a modicum of social solidarity** lose that solidarity in the transition — and the loss is not recorded on any of Coleman's indicators.

**Criticism three attacks its conception of the person.** Under a profit logic, even **higher education, art and culture** become geared to **market needs**, and human beings are **reduced to self-seeking animals**. The printed trade-off closes the block: material welfare is gained **at the expense of their moral strength**.

⚠️ Keeping the three distinct is the whole technique here. An answer that merges them into "the market model is Western and materialistic" has produced one weak objection where the chapter supplies three strong and independent ones.

### Deployment in GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay

- **GS-II.** On stems about growth-led strategies, catch-up or the costs of rapid transition, criticism one is the analytical objection and criticisms two and three supply the social and human costs without requiring any data.
- **GS-IV.** The reduction to **self-seeking animals** and the moral-strength trade-off are the strongest available statements of the claim that economic arrangements shape character — the premise behind every ethics question about commercialisation.
- **Essay.** Frank's question is a ready thesis sentence for prompts on imitation, catch-up or the universality of development models.

**Answer framework — the model-critique paragraph, in four moves.** State the model with its equation and one stage list, so the critique has a target → attack the history first with the starting-points question, because it is the only criticism that denies the model's feasibility rather than its desirability → add the solidarity and person-level costs as independent objections, labelling them as such → close on the printed trade-off, which converts the criticisms into a single evaluative claim. ⚠️ Beginning with the moral objections and ending with history reverses the argumentative weight; feasibility must come first.

Revision notes:

- The equation: **development = modernization = Westernization** — liberal-democratic politics, competitive markets, equality of opportunity with respect for **merit**.
- **Coleman's modern society:** **urbanisation, widespread literacy, high per-capita income, geographical and social mobility, commercialisation and industrialisation, penetrative mass communication, widespread participation**.
- Growth is treated as a **necessary** condition.
- **Rostow's five stages:** traditional · preconditions for take-off · take-off · drive to maturity · high mass consumption.
- **Organski's four stages:** primitive unification · industrialisation · national welfare · politics of abundance.
- 🔑 **Frank (1971):** Western underdevelopment was **original**; developing-country underdevelopment is a **consequence** — **How can they follow the same pattern of development when their starting points are radically different?** Proposition proved: stage theories smuggle in a **false analogy**.
- Communitarian objection: societies that **compensated for their material poverty by a modicum of social solidarity** lose it.
- Profit objection: **higher education, art and culture** geared to **market needs**; people **reduced to self-seeking animals**.
- Printed trade-off: material welfare gained **at the expense of their moral strength**.
- ⚠️ The three criticisms attack **history, social cost and the person** — keep them separate.
- ⚠️ Coleman's checklist is the step-one specification Lesson 15 identified as the method's vulnerability.

### Concept check

**Question:** State the market-society model and the three printed criticisms of it, showing that each attacks something different.

**Model answer:** The model equates **development with modernization and modernization with Westernization** — liberal-democratic structures politically, competitive markets economically, and equality of opportunity with respect for **merit** socially. **Coleman's** definition of a modern society supplies the target: **urbanisation, widespread literacy, high per-capita income, geographical and social mobility, commercialisation and industrialisation, penetrative mass communication and widespread participation**. Growth is a **necessary condition**, with **Rostow's five stages** — traditional, preconditions for take-off, take-off, drive to maturity, high mass consumption — and **Organski's four** — primitive unification, industrialisation, national welfare, politics of abundance. ⚠️ Three criticisms follow and they are not variants of one complaint. **The first attacks its history. Frank (1971)** holds that Western underdevelopment was an **original** condition whereas developing-country underdevelopment is a **consequence** of European capitalist expansion, and asks: **How can they follow the same pattern of development when their starting points are radically different?** 🔑 That denies the model's feasibility, not its desirability, and it is the strongest of the three. **The second attacks its social cost**: societies that **compensated for their material poverty by a modicum of social solidarity** forfeit that solidarity in the transition, a loss invisible on every one of Coleman's indicators. **The third attacks its conception of the person**: under profit logic even **higher education, art and culture** are geared to **market needs**, and human beings are **reduced to self-seeking animals**. The block closes on the printed trade-off — material welfare gained **at the expense of their moral strength**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not compress the three criticisms into a single charge of Western bias. The history objection is a claim about availability of the path; the solidarity objection is a claim about uncounted costs; the profit objection is a claim about what people become. Merging them produces a rhetorical complaint where the chapter supplies three independent arguments, and it also loses the strongest of them, since only the first denies that the sequence could be followed at all.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Stage theories of development assume a path that was never available." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (within 150 words):** The proposition is **Frank's (1971)** objection stated as a thesis. **The case for.** Stage theories — **Rostow's** five from traditional to **high mass consumption**, **Organski's** four from primitive unification to the **politics of abundance** — describe one sequence and treat departures from it as delay. But Western underdevelopment was an **original** condition, while developing-country underdevelopment is a **consequence** of European capitalist expansion: **How can they follow the same pattern of development when their starting points are radically different?** **Baran (1957)** sharpens it — the original accumulation ran through colonies, which cannot be replicated. **The case against.** The sequence also describes genuine functional changes — **differentiation**, the move from **ascribed to achieved** status — that are not obviously path-dependent. ⚠️ Verdict: the stages describe real transitions and misdescribe their availability, which is why the printed remedy is **borrowed plus indigenous technology** rather than either imitation or refusal.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks: 3 + 3 + 2 + 2):** 3 for stating at least one stage list accurately in sequence, since the objection is about sequence and an answer without one has nothing to attack; 3 for Frank's original-against-consequence distinction, with the starting-points question used as evidence rather than as a flourish; 2 for a genuine counter-case conceding that some transitions described are real; 2 for a verdict distinguishing **description** from **availability** and landing on the chapter's own middle remedy. Deduct 2 for merging the three printed criticisms into one charge of Western bias, and 2 for naming any present-day country as an instance of a stage.
