# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

Four checks spanning the arc: three consolidate a stage and the fourth spans the whole subject. Each model is held within 250 words.

### Cumulative check A — Stage One, Lessons 1 to 9

**Question:** Trace the chain from the elite challenge to deliberative democracy, naming at each step what the previous answer could not supply.

**Model answer:** One: elite theory asserts that a minority always rules — **Mosca's** rulers and ruled, **Michels's iron law of oligarchy**, **Pareto's** masses who fear responsibility. Two: democratic elitism accepts it and redefines democracy as **a competitive struggle for the people's votes** (Schumpeter), conditioned by **merit selection** and a **shortened distance** (Mannheim), a **divided** elite (Aron), and leadership as **the core of the democratic system** against **anti-democratic counter-elites** (Sartori) — but it cannot supply dispersion of power. Three: pluralism supplies many minorities, **Dahl's polyarchy** on the **Bentley–Truman–Latham** lineage, with policy as bargaining — but it cannot supply equal organisational capacity, which is why **producers beat consumers** and both theories **tend to maintain the status quo**. Four: participatory democracy supplies agency through nine printed devices and three justifications — but it cannot supply equal voice, since crowd-mobilisers secure **unreasonable demands** while modest petitioners **go unheard**. Five: the Marxist critique supplies the structural ground — political power as the **handmaid of economic power**, **false consent**, Lenin's periodicity — but its alternative is judged **by no means an example of democracy**, with the commune failing through **the abolition of incentives for better work**. Six: Macpherson supplies a criterion, **developmental** against **extractive power**, pursued in three spheres **simultaneously** — but **Norman Barry** objects that the comparison is with an **ideal version**. Seven: deliberative democracy supplies the missing condition, **mutual persuasion through reasoned argument**, with representatives obliged to **report and justify… regularly**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read the chain as a progression in which each theory corrects its predecessor and the last one wins. The chapter appraises each separately and its sharpest objection — the mobilisation asymmetry — attacks the model most often recommended as the remedy. The correct reading is that each supplies one condition and none supplies all.

### Cumulative check B — Stage Two, Lessons 10 to 14

**Question:** Move from the vocabulary of change to the reform-versus-revolution verdict, showing where the mechanism is supplied and where it is attacked.

**Model answer:** The vocabulary comes first: **revolution** and **evolution** differ on **pace**, **development** and **progress** on **measure**, since the first three are scaled empirically and progress holds a form **morally superior**. Six transition pairs — **Comte**, **Spencer**, **Maine**, **Tönnies**, **Durkheim**, **Weber** — give its direction. The mechanism is dialectical: Marx retains Hegel's engine and reverses its substrate, and **Engels's three laws** make it operative — **quantity into quality** (water to **ice** or **steam**) explains abruptness from gradual causes; **interpenetration of opposites** (**hard iron that can be moulded**) makes contradiction internal; **negation of negation** (**seed → ear of corn**, reproducing **in larger quantity and better quality**) makes the outcome an advance. Historical materialism applies it: **forces** against **relations of production**, a **legal and political superstructure** rising on the economic structure, and the fetters passage — **Then begins an epoch of social revolution** — with **the hand-mill gives you society with the feudal lord**. **Sabine** corrects the reading: 1789 was a **political** revolution completing an economic change. The theory of revolution adds necessity — **revolution is the indispensable midwife of social change** — and finality through the **majority against the minority** argument, which **Mao's permanent revolution** disowns from within. ⚠️ Popper attacks the form: historicism is a **pseudo-science** because **insulated from falsification**; knowledge cannot be predicted; concentration of capital is **a tendency, not a law**; so **piecemeal** engineering is preferred. Yet **Sorel** denies that **gradual reform** achieves anything lasting, and **Kafka** that revolution leaves anything but **the slime of a new bureaucracy**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not conclude this stage by choosing reform. The chapter's appraisal is explicitly two-sided and ends by stating what each route must guarantee — that no **new privileged class in a new garb** appears, and that reform does not **evaporate** but promotes **equality of opportunity** and reduced disparities of power, prestige and wealth.

### Cumulative check C — Stage Three, Lessons 15 to 21

**Question:** Run the development argument from its conception to its sequencing conclusion, naming the rival at each stage.

**Model answer:** The conception comes first: development is **more satisfying in terms of human wants and aspirations** on an **empirical** scale, defined by **Mittelman** as **increasing capacity**, by **Baran** as a **far-reaching transformation** that **has never been a smooth, harmonious process**, and by **Rodney** as **a many-sided process** including freedom, creativity, **self-discipline** and responsibility; underdevelopment is a **blockage**, not a backlog. The **third world** is **Sauvy's (1952)** category on the third-estate parallel, which **should have become outdated** yet survives **due to long usage**. Two explanations then compete. The liberal account treats underdevelopment as an earlier stage marked by **differentiation**, national loyalty and **ascribed → achieved** status, with **Bryce**, **Friedrich**, **Almond and Coleman**, **Almond and Powell** and **Pye** as exponents. The dependency account treats it as produced: **Lenin (1916)**, **Baran (1957)**, and **Frank's (1967) centre–periphery** model in which the centre's development **produces** the periphery's underdevelopment, surviving independence — hence **delinking**, with **Amin** and **Unequal Exchange**. Four paths follow: the **market** model with **Rostow's** five and **Organski's** four stages, attacked by **Frank's** starting-points question; the **welfare** model from **Bismarck** through **Asquith** to **Beveridge**, with the **cradle to the grave** architecture and a ceiling of **capacity**; the **socialist** model, delivering employment and health **although they hardly care to protect civil liberties**; and the **Gandhian** model, which changes the criterion. The **ecological** correction applies to all four, three being **Indifferent** to nature. Finally **Kothari (1976)** refutes the modernise-first thesis: European **national consolidation preceded modernisation**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the four paths as four positions on one scale of state involvement. Three can be arranged that way and the Gandhian cannot, because it changes the criterion rather than the dosage — which is why a rise in output can count against it and why it alone appears as **Very friendly** to nature.

### Cumulative check D — the whole subject, Lessons 1 to 23

**Question:** In one connected argument, move from "who rules?" to a graded verdict on democracy and development, marking where each stage does its work.

**Model answer:** Stage One establishes that a minority always decides and that the democratic question is therefore about selection, constraint and replacement (L1), that dispersion among groups does not equalise voice (L2), that participation supplies agency but allocates influence by mobilisation capacity (L3), that the structural critique locates the defect in ownership and in **false consent** (L4–L6), that the proposed alternative is **by no means an example of democracy** (L7), that the missing criterion is **developmental power** (L8), and that the missing condition is **mutual persuasion through reasoned argument** with continuous justification (L9). Stage Two supplies the vocabulary of change and the empirical/normative split (L10), the mechanism in **Engels's three laws** (L11), the structural application through base, superstructure and the fetters passage (L12), the necessity and finality claims (L13), and their refutation plus the two-sided caution that neither route is self-justifying (L14). Stage Three fixes the conception of development as capacity, structure and person (L15), sets the liberal against the dependency explanation (L16), and ranges four paths with different criteria (L17–L19) under one ecological limit (L20), closing on capabilities, the **balance rule**, nation-building, state-building and **Kothari's** sequencing refutation (L21). The post-source extension adds populism, backsliding, ecological political theory and digital democracy as labelled contemporary vocabulary (L22). ⚠️ The verdict is then graded (L23): democracy is **partly constitutive** of development on the printed definitions, and the real question is which features of democratic practice build development and which erode it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not end the subject by selecting a model of democracy or a path of development. Every theory in these three chapters carries a printed objection, including the last ones, and the chapters' own method is appraisal rather than endorsement. The graded close names conditions and failure modes; ❌ it names no country, and it has nowhere further to point, because this is the final topic of the source and of the subject.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ Original practice prompts written for this edition. They are **not** previous-year questions and must not be cited as such. Fifteen- and twenty-mark models are held within 250 words.

**Practice question 1 [10]:** Distinguish democratic elitism from pluralism as responses to the elite challenge. *(Distinguish — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** Both accept that a minority decides; they differ on how many minorities there are and on what disciplines them. **Democratic elitism** keeps a single political elite and relocates democracy into the manner of its selection and removal: democracy becomes **a competitive struggle for the people's votes**, conditioned by **merit selection** and a **shortened distance** (Mannheim), by a **divided** rather than unified elite producing government as **a business of compromise** (Aron), and by the warning that the danger is the **absence** of leadership and the rise of **anti-democratic counter-elites** (Sartori). **Pluralism** denies there is one elite at all: on the **Bentley–Truman–Latham** lineage and **Dahl's polyarchy**, policy is bargaining whose outcome tracks group **strength and intensity**, so far that **the form of government becomes almost insignificant**. ⚠️ The chapter convicts both of one fault — they **tend to maintain the status quo**, since **producers beat consumers** by organisation.

**Scoring note:** the marks are in the discipline each proposes — competitive selection against dispersed bargaining — not in listing thinkers. The shared status-quo verdict is the cheapest available third paragraph and most answers omit it.

**Practice question 2 [10]:** Why does the chapter attach a limit to participatory democracy, and what does the limit actually claim? *(Answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** The limit is attached because participation allocates influence unequally. The positive case is strong: against the **Schumpeter–Dahl axis**, which reduces democracy **from a humanist aspiration to a market equilibrium system**, participation restores agency through **contact, initiative, recall, public hearing, advisory council, referendum, protest, civil disobedience** and **political violence**, justified **instrumentally**, **developmentally** — it **enhances moral, social and political awareness** — and **communitarianly**. The limit then states that participation needs **concrete action, not a theory**, that benefits **come very late**, and that excess produces strikes, picketing, **gheraos** and damage to public property. 🔑 Its real claim is distributive, not about order: **those who have the skill to gather and mobilize crowds** secure **unreasonable demands** while those presenting a case **peacefully and modestly will go unheard**. ⚠️ So participation reproduces the organisational asymmetry that pluralism was already convicted of.

**Scoring note:** the discriminator is reading the objection as distributive rather than as a law-and-order worry. An answer that treats it as a complaint about protest cannot connect it to the deliberative remedy, which is where the argument goes next.

**Practice question 3 [15]:** "Reform is safer, revolution is deeper; neither is sufficient." Critically examine. *(Critically examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The proposition is the chapter's own two-sided appraisal, and examining it means stating each side at full strength before the qualification. **Revolution's depth.** Change accumulates gradually and synthesises abruptly — the first dialectical law, water becoming **ice** or **steam** at a threshold — so **any significant social change is always the product of a revolution** and **revolution is the indispensable midwife of social change**. The reason is power: the dominant class holds a legitimating ideology and never yields until forced, which makes **social revolution the necessary lever of social change**. **Reform's safety.** Historicism is a **pseudo-science** because **insulated from falsification**; knowledge grows, influences history and cannot be predicted; Marx's concentration of capital is **a tendency, not a law**. Hence **piecemeal** over **Utopian** engineering, on three grounds — no agreement exists on a remote ideal, so imposing it demands **authoritarian means and violence**; the present generation bears costs for a later one; and reform should proceed by **step-by-step approximation**. The question is reframed: not **who should rule** but **how to minimise the risk of bad rulers**. **Why neither suffices.** **Sorel** holds that **gradual reform never really achieves anything in the long run**; **Kafka** holds that **every revolution evaporates and only leaves behind the slime of a new bureaucracy**. ⚠️ Verdict: each is defensible only with a guarantee attached — no **new privileged class in a new garb**, and no effect allowed to **evaporate** without **equality of opportunity** and reduced disparities of power, prestige and wealth.

**Scoring note:** the top band requires that the falsifiability objection be stated as a charge about the **form** of the theory, not about failed predictions. The Sorel–Kafka pair must be used symmetrically; using only Kafka converts a balanced appraisal into an anti-revolutionary answer the chapter does not support.

**Practice question 4 [15]:** Examine whether a formally democratic system can be called democratic while economic power remains concentrated. *(Examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The question is the chapter's central dispute and it has three serious positions. **The negative case.** Political power is the **handmaid of economic power**, so the class character of the state does not turn on election results; **Plamenatz's** four reasons show how wealth converts into power through schooling, organisation, elite attitudes and control of **information**; **Maguire (1978)** adds that the state represents workers **only in an illusory manner**, generating **false consent**; and **Lenin (1917)** reduces the franchise to deciding **every few years** which representatives will **represent and repress**. On the comparison axes, the elitists' conditions — an **open elite system** and **periodic elections on universal suffrage** — are answered as **an eyewash**. **The affirmative case.** The chapter's own pivot notes that if the four reasons were the only grounds, equalising within capitalism would make the model genuinely democratic — and the structural alternative fared worse on the democratic test itself: a narrow construction of **the people**, no recognition of the **expanding middle class**, curbed opposition, and a verdict that it **may be more conducive to social justice, but it is by no means an example of democracy**. **The middle.** Gramsci relocates the problem from form to consent, explaining **the incredible resilience of the bourgeois civilization**, while Macpherson supplies the remedy as **developmental power** pursued **simultaneously** in three spheres. ⚠️ Verdict: formal democracy is genuine as procedure and incomplete as self-rule; the defect lies not in the form but in what the form leaves undisturbed.

**Scoring note:** the discriminator is the pivot. An answer that only stacks the critique has argued for redistribution without noticing that the chapter treats redistribution as insufficient; an answer that only defends the form has not engaged **false consent**. Both the concession and the printed verdict on the alternative are required for the top band.

**Practice question 5 [20]:** "Development is a contested criterion, not a measurable quantity." Critically examine with reference to rival paths of development. *(Critically examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The proposition is half true, and the half it gets wrong matters. **Where it is wrong.** Development is explicitly measurable on an **empirical** scale — **stronger, more organized, more efficient, more effective**, and **more satisfying in terms of human wants and aspirations** — which is precisely what distinguishes it from **progress**, whose criterion is **normative**. The two-step method, identifying an **advanced society** and measuring distance from it, is a measuring procedure. **Where it is right.** The procedure's first step fixes a model, and the models disagree. **Market**: development is **modernization** and modernization **Westernization**, with **Rostow's five** and **Organski's four** stages, criticised by **Frank (1971)** — **How can they follow the same pattern of development when their starting points are radically different?** — and for a welfare gained **at the expense of their moral strength**. **Welfare**: provision reconstructed as **mutual assistance and self-reliance**, limited by capacity to a **subdued scale**. **Socialist**: employment, education and health care, **although they hardly care to protect civil liberties and political rights**. **Gandhian**: the criterion itself changes to **need but not their greed**, **bread labour** and **production by the masses**. The printed table confirms the divergence at a single cell: three paths **Indifferent** to nature, the Gandhian **Very friendly**. ⚠️ Verdict: development is measurable **once a criterion is chosen**, and the choice is contested — which is why the chapter prints four paths and a corrective rather than one scale, and why **Schumacher's** accounting objection shows a measure can be wrong at source.

**Scoring note:** a *critically examine* directive requires the proposition's false half to be conceded first, using the empirical/normative split, before the contestation is demonstrated. The table's nature row is the cheapest proof of genuine divergence; answers that list four models without showing that their criteria differ have illustrated rather than examined.

**Practice question 6 [20]:** Discuss whether political development can be pursued independently of nation-building and state-building. *(Discuss — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The chapter answers no, and its reasons are worth separating from its conclusion. **What political development requires.** It is the transformation of institutions so a system can **meet changing needs**, and both printed models specify it. **Coleman and Pye** give **differentiation**, **equality** — **the ethos of modernity**, in which **subjects** become **citizens** — and **capacity**, together the **development syndrome**. **Almond and Powell** give structural differentiation across **input** functions (socialisation, articulation, aggregation, communication) and **output** functions (rule-making, rule-application, rule-adjudication), the secularisation of culture from **parochial** to **subject** to **participant**, and four capabilities — **regulative, extractive, distributive, responsive** — under a rule that the first two be **matched** by the last two. **Why the two buildings are prior.** Nation-building transfers loyalty from tribe, village or petty principality to the central system, producing **identity, allegiance, emotional bond and solidarity**; state-building evolves a **common centre of power** extending law, order, defence and welfare to **every part** of the territory. Differentiation presupposes a system to differentiate within, and responsive capability presupposes a public that recognises the system as its own. **The refutation that settles it.** Against the modernise-first thesis, **Kothari (1976)** observes that **European history does not corroborate it**, since European countries achieved **national consolidation before embarking on modernisation**. ⚠️ Add the anti-convergence caution: requisites must be combined **with its own genius**. Verdict: not independent, and not merely sequenced — complementary and simultaneous.

**Scoring note:** the *discuss* directive requires both models before the conclusion, and the marks concentrate in two places: the balance rule, which converts capability talk into a criterion, and Kothari's European premise, which converts a recommendation into an argument. An answer giving Kothari's conclusion without the premise forfeits the strongest available close.

# REMEDIATION

Fourteen errors that recur when this topic is used in a Mains answer.

| # | Error | Why it fails | Repair | Lesson |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | Denying that a minority rules, instead of conceding it | The organisational argument is structural, and no theory in these chapters disputes it; denial forfeits the ground on which the democratic reply is built | Concede the premise, then convert it into criteria — openness, removability, genuine competition, shortened distance | L1 |
| R2 | Flattening the elite-theory ordering into "Pareto founded elite theory" or "Mosca came first" | The text credits Pareto with first using the **terms** and Mosca (1896) and Michels (1911) with the earlier **idea**; both flattenings misreport it | Reproduce the formulation as printed, in one clause | L1 |
| R3 | Caricaturing Schumpeter as anti-democratic | He redefines democracy procedurally as **a competitive struggle for the people's votes**; the redefinition retains elections as the real constraint | Give the definition and its two implications, then let Macpherson supply the objection | L1, L8 |
| R4 | Treating this chapter's pluralism as the pluralist theory of sovereignty | The text says the sense is **sociological**; importing sovereignty pluralism answers a different question | Name the sense once, then run the group lineage and the producers-over-consumers asymmetry | L2 |
| R5 | Recommending "more participation" without the mobilisation asymmetry | The printed objection is distributive — crowd-mobilisers secure **unreasonable demands**, modest petitioners **go unheard** — so unqualified participation recommends a mechanism with a known failure mode | Attach the asymmetry, then show that deliberation supplies the missing criterion | L3, L9 |
| R6 | Giving Plamenatz's four reasons as the Marxist critique | The chapter's pivot shows those four are compatible with a reformist conclusion; the Marxist ground is structural | Give the four, then the pivot, then the three printed mechanisms | L4 |
| R7 | Explaining the commune's failure by repression | The printed reason is **the abolition of incentives for better work** — an economic explanation of a political failure | Reproduce the printed reason; make the repression argument separately, where it is actually printed | L5, L7 |
| R8 | Quoting democratic centralism's first element without its second | Element (b) imposes the highest body's decision **rigidly at all lower levels**, which cancels much of (a)'s upward selection | Always give both elements; the pair is what generates the later objection | L5 |
| R9 | Using Gramsci only as a critique of liberal democracy | In this chapter hegemony is the **third objection to Marxist democracy**: it explains why the revolution did not arrive and reschedules it to civil society | Use both functions — manufactured consent, and the rescheduled revolution | L7 |
| R10 | Attributing deliberative democracy to Habermas on this window | The printed exponents are **Walzer, Cohen and Rogers, Manin, Hurley, Fishkin**; Habermas is not among them | Keep one flagged bridge mention with its pointer, and attribute the model to the printed exponents | L9 |
| R11 | Using "progress" as a neutral descriptive term, or reducing development to output | Progress carries a **normative** judgment; development is defined by capacity, structure and the person, not by GDP | Fix the measure before arguing; cite Mittelman, Baran or Rodney as the stem requires | L10, L15 |
| R12 | Writing "Earth Summit, Stockholm 1972", or presenting dated consumption figures as current | The 1972 Stockholm meeting was the **UN Conference on the Human Environment**; the consumption figures are a 1985 source reported in a 2009 text | Use the correct name; attribute figures to their source and date them; route live data elsewhere | L20 |
| R13 | Presenting Gandhi as anti-development or anti-machine | The printed position concedes that **machinery has its place; it has come to stay**, objecting only where it **displaces necessary human labour** | Give the displacement test, then the author's own qualification about feasibility and sustainability | L19 |
| R14 | Listing the four capabilities without the balance rule, or giving Kothari's conclusion without his premise | The examinable content is the **matching** requirement and the **European-history** evidence; without them the first is vocabulary and the second a preference | Test the balance; attach the premise that consolidation preceded modernisation | L21 |

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Map 1 — The five models of democracy, scored on what each supplies and what it cannot

| | **Elitist / democratic-elitist** | **Pluralist** | **Participatory** | **Marxist / substantive** | **Deliberative** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Who holds effective power** | A selected elite, in principle open | Many organised minorities — **polyarchy** | Citizens acting directly through printed devices | The owning class, whatever the form | Citizens plus accountable professional politicians |
| **Test of democracy** | Open entry, genuine competition, real removal | Dispersion of influence across contending groups | Breadth and depth of actual involvement | Transformation of the mode of production | Whether decisions can be **justified** to those bound by them |
| **The sentence that carries it** | **A competitive struggle for the people's votes**; the elite is **the core of the democratic system** | **The form of government becomes almost insignificant** | Democracy reduced **from a humanist aspiration to a market equilibrium system** | Elections without transformed production are **an eyewash** | Representatives must **report and justify their decisions and actions to the people regularly** |
| **What it supplies that the one before cannot** | A testable criterion where "rule by the people" had failed | Dispersion, so power is not single | Agency, so citizens are not only choosers | A material account of why form is insufficient | A standard of reasons, which none of the others contains |
| **What it cannot supply** | Dispersion or agency | Equal organisational capacity | Equal voice — mobilisation capacity decides | A democratic form: **by no means an example of democracy** | The material conditions of equal deliberation |
| **Printed objection** | Rule by the able **corresponds to Aristotle's defence of slavery** | It **tends to maintain the status quo** | Crowd-mobilisers win **unreasonable demands**; modest petitioners **go unheard** | Narrow construction of **the people**; no **expanding middle class**; curbed opposition | Presumes a culture of argument, information and listening |

⚠️ The two rows that decide a comparison are **what it supplies** and **what it cannot**. An answer that contrasts conclusions has compared five verdicts; an answer that contrasts supplies and deficits has explained why the chapter needs five models and endorses none.

## Map 2 — The causal chain of change, from mechanism to verdict

```text
  STAGE TWO AS ONE CHAIN -- each arrow is a printed step

  FOUR CONCEPTS fixed on a 2x2  [pace x measure]
        |
        v
  DIALECTIC inverted: engine from Hegel, substrate from Marx
        |
        +--> LAW 1 quantity -> quality .... WHY change is ABRUPT
        +--> LAW 2 interpenetration ....... WHY contradiction is INTERNAL
        +--> LAW 3 negation of negation ... WHY outcome is an ADVANCE
        |
        v
  HISTORICAL MATERIALISM
     forces of production develop
        -> relations become FETTERS
        -> 'Then begins an EPOCH OF SOCIAL REVOLUTION'
        -> four epochs, each with its contending pair
        -> SABINE: 1789 was POLITICAL -- it REGISTERED a changed base
        |
        v
  THEORY OF REVOLUTION
     power is never surrendered -> revolution is the NECESSARY LEVER
     MAJORITY vs MINORITY -> therefore the FINAL revolution
        |                        ^
        |                        |  CONTRADICTED FROM INSIDE by
        |                        +-- MAO'S PERMANENT REVOLUTION
        v
  POPPER'S REFUTATION
     attacks the FORM, not the content: insulated from FALSIFICATION
     -> knowledge cannot be predicted
     -> laws only for fragmented, isolated units
     -> concentration of capital = A TENDENCY
     -> PIECEMEAL over UTOPIAN engineering
     -> reframe: HOW TO MINIMISE BAD RULERS
        |
        v
  THE TWO-SIDED VERDICT
     SOREL: gradual reform achieves nothing lasting
     KAFKA: every revolution leaves 'the slime of a new bureaucracy'
     => NEITHER ROUTE IS SELF-JUSTIFYING; each needs its guarantee
```

## Map 3 — The four development paths against the ecological corrective

| | **Market** | **Welfare** | **Socialist** | **Gandhian** | **Ecological corrective** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Criterion of success** | Growth, mobility, high mass consumption | Security within a market order | Material equality through social ownership | Restrained need, dignified labour, non-violence | Needs met without compromising future generations |
| **Signature evidence** | **Rostow's** five stages; **Organski's** four; Coleman's modern society | **Bismarck → Asquith (1911) → Beveridge (1942)**; **from the cradle to the grave** | Employment, education, health care **although they hardly care to protect civil liberties** | **I call it satanic**; **need but not their greed**; **production by the masses** | **Brundtland (1987)**; Schumacher's **eating away its own capital** |
| **Printed objection** | Starting points **radically different**; lost solidarity; people **reduced to self-seeking animals** | Built **only on a subdued scale**; bureaucratic inefficiency and corruption | Civil and political rights compressed; decline through shortage and **the mad rush of demands for subsidies and reservations** | Halting industrialisation may not be feasible | Growth remains necessary for poverty eradication, which the **needs** emphasis retains |
| **Attitude toward nature** | **Indifferent** | **Indifferent** | **Indifferent** | **Very friendly** | The axis on which the other four are judged |
| **Its one-line answer to "what is development for?"** | Enlarging what people can buy | Securing what people cannot lose | Equalising what people own | Restraining what people want | Preserving what people must pass on |

🔑 The bottom row is the fastest route into any development stem: five different answers to one question, which is precisely why the question cannot be settled by a single indicator.

## Map 4 — The eight most answer-worthy quotations, each paired with the proposition it proves

| Quotation | Proposition it proves | Deploy at |
|---|---|---|
| **The leaders trade in votes and the better combination of policies will fetch more votes** | Procedural democracy is a market, so its defects are market defects | L1, and any critique of electoral politics |
| **Those who have the skill to gather and mobilize crowds will be able to secure fulfilment of their unreasonable demands… go unheard** | Participation allocates influence by mobilisation capacity | L3, L23 |
| **Allowed every few years to decide which particular representatives of the oppressing classes shall represent and repress them** | Periodicity without power | L4, answered at L9 |
| **Perhaps more rigidly than… under the Czarist regime** | The transitional phase did not transit | L7 |
| **He evaluates existing liberal democracy by reference to some 'ideal version'… rather than… existing alternatives** | Ideal-versus-actual comparison is a method error | L8, and any utopian proposal |
| **Every revolution evaporates and only leaves behind the slime of a new bureaucracy** | The revolution side's weakest point — paired with **Sorel** for the reform side's | L14 |
| **How can they follow the same pattern of development when their starting points are radically different?** | Stage theories smuggle in a false analogy | L17 |
| **The modern industrial system… consumes the very basis on which it has been erected** | Growth that draws down capital is mis-measured at source | L20 |

⚠️ Pairing is the technique. A quotation deployed without the proposition it proves is decoration; the same quotation attached to a claim is evidence, and only the second earns marks.

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

Compressed recall for all twenty-three lessons, under topic-specific headings. ⚠️ Read under the three standing qualifications stated at the opening: book-period edition, grounded attribution, terminal boundary.

## 1. The elite premise and the four revisions

- **Elite/mass division** asserted as permanent. **Mosca (1896)**: rulers and ruled, the ruled incompetent. **Michels (1911)**: **iron law of oligarchy**; the majority apathetic, indolent, **permanently incapable of self-government**. **Pareto**: elites have ability, masses fear responsibility.
- 📝 Ordering: Pareto first used the **terms**; Mosca and Michels had the **idea** earlier. ❌ Do not flatten.
- **Mannheim**: removal or pressure always available; conditions — **merit selection**, **shortened distance**.
- **Schumpeter**: democracy is **that institutional arrangement… by means of a competitive struggle for the people's votes**; people choose, do not rule; **leaders trade in votes**.
- **Aron**: unified against **divided** elite; government as **a business of compromise**.
- **Sartori**: the elite is **the core of the democratic system**; the danger is the **absence** of leadership and **anti-democratic counter-elites**.
- The surrender's terms: rule by the people survives as a **standard**, not a description.

## 2. Group theory, polyarchy and the status-quo verdict

- Two categories: elitist-democratic (Mannheim, Aron) and **group theories**.
- **Bentley (1908) · Truman (1951) · Latham (1952) · Dahl (1956) → polyarchy**.
- 📝 *Pluralism* here is **sociological**, ❌ not the pluralist theory of sovereignty.
- Policy as decentralised bargaining tracking **strength and intensity**; corollary — **the form of government becomes almost insignificant**; illustration — **producers beat consumers** because better organised.
- Appraisal of elitism **and** pluralism together: social inequalities do not track natural ones; domination is **closely related to economic disparities**; the theory **tends to maintain the status quo**; counterfactual — rewards tracking the **quality and amount of work** would dissolve domination.
- Widening distance between people and representatives in **large democracies like India and the United States**.

## 3. Participation: devices, justifications and the mobilisation asymmetry

- **Macpherson's diagnosis**: the **Schumpeter–Dahl axis** reduces democracy **from a humanist aspiration to a market equilibrium system**.
- **Rousseau**: sovereignty **retained** and inalienable; deputies are **agents, not representatives**; government revocable.
- Axes: **conventional/unconventional**; **citizen-initiated/government-initiated**.
- Nine devices: **contact · initiative · recall · public hearing · advisory council · referendum · protest · civil disobedience · political violence**, including **passive resistance and satyagraha**.
- Three justifications: **instrumental · developmental/educational · communitarian**.
- Means: **panchayati raj in India**; **Switzerland's** referendum; **Australia's compulsory constitutional referendum** — combining direct with representative democracy.
- Socialist variant: *Vanguard of the Proletariat*; **Mao's mass line**. Epigraph: **extraordinary possibilities in ordinary people** (Fosdick).
- 🔑 Limit: **concrete action, not a theory**; benefits **come very late**; **gheraos** and disruption; crowd-mobilisers secure **unreasonable demands** while modest petitioners **go unheard**.

## 4. The Marxist critique: four reasons, one pivot, three mechanisms

- Premise: the executive is **a committee for managing the common affairs of the whole bourgeoisie**.
- **Plamenatz (1978)**: wealth buys **schooling**; large organisations shift power to **leaders**; leaders of modest origin acquire **privileged attitudes**; power depends on **information**, which the wealthy control.
- 🔑 **The pivot**: were those the only grounds, equalising inside capitalism would make liberal democracy true — refused, because the target is the **system**.
- (i) **Handmaid of economic power**. (ii) **Maguire (1978)** — representation **in an illusory manner**, the workers' **ideological misconception of themselves** → **false consent**. (iii) Suffrage conceded once **property relations were safe**; **Lenin (1917)** — **every few years… represent and repress**.

## 5. The alternative: two phases, one commune, one distinction

- **All state power implies dictatorship**; bourgeois democracy and dictatorship **coterminous**.
- **Lefebvre**: the dictatorship of the proletariat is **concrete democracy, i.e. the coercive power of a majority over a minority** — judged by **purpose, not form**.
- **Democratic centralism**: (a) each body elected by the **lower body**; (b) the highest body's decision **imposed rigidly at all lower levels**.
- Transitional phase: Roman **dictatura**; the state **withers away**; **simple administrative functions** (*Capital* Vol.3, 1894).
- **Commune**: **pyramidal structure of direct democracy**, delegates **bound by instructions and removable**; failed in the USSR and China **primarily due to the abolition of incentives for better work**.
- **"People's democracy" is tautological**, adopted to claim **substantive** against **procedural** democracy; the roster collapsed in **1989–91**.

## 6. Marxist against elitist: the five axes and the table

- Shared premise: neither believes a democratic **form** secures political equality.
- Axes: **domination · dominant class · social division · justice and rationality · scope of social change**, plus **scope of democracy**.
- Elitists' conditions: **open elite system** + **periodic elections on universal suffrage**; Marxist reply — **an eyewash**.
- 🔑 Printed analogy: elitism **corresponds to Aristotle's defence of slavery**.
- Ladder: **capitalism → socialism → communism**; **to each according to his work** → **to each according to his need**.
- *Circulation of Elites*: **lions** and **foxes**; **no possibility of the rule of the elite being ever replaced by the rule of the masses**.
- The exact disagreement: the structure **rotates** against the structure **terminates**.

## 7. The appraisal of Marxist democracy, and hegemony

- Concession first: the critique **forced liberal democracy to accommodate working-class claims** to keep its legitimacy.
- Objection 1: narrow **people**; no rights for capitalists; 🔑 **no recognition of the expanding middle class**; opposition curbed → **may be more conducive to social justice, but it is by no means an example of democracy**.
- Objection 2: **USSR 1917**, **PRC 1949**, the **Sino-Soviet dispute of the 1960s**; **MacIver (1965)** — a pyramid **perhaps more rigidly than… under the Czarist regime**; **glasnost** → **1991**.
- Objection 3: **Gramsci** — coercion **matched by** cultural and moral leadership; **the incredible resilience of the bourgeois civilization**; **undermine the hegemony of bourgeois values before seeking a political takeover**.
- ⚠️ Hegemony is printed as an objection to the **Marxist road**, not only to liberal democracy.

## 8. Macpherson's four pairs and the three spheres

- 1966: three variants — liberal needing **a more humane touch**; communist **if** intra-party democracy and open bureaucracies; Third World by **mass enthusiasm**.
- 1973: attacks **Schumpeter, Dahl and Bentham**; refuses **whatever works, is right**; **the cry of the oppressed** against **political consumers in the political market**.
- **Utility-maximization** (consumer) against **power-maximization** (doer and creator, via **J. S. Mill**).
- 🔑 **Developmental power** against **extractive power**.
- Simple exchange against capitalist economy — **labour becomes a commodity**; **possessive individualism**, **owing nothing to society**.
- Conclusion: socialist ownership + liberal institutions; welfare by **need rather than desert**; **a difficult proposition indeed**; three spheres **simultaneously**.
- **Norman Barry (1989)**: judging the real against an **ideal version** rather than **existing alternatives**.

## 9. Deliberation as the missing condition

- Reconciles **democracy as popular rule** with **democracy as the bulwark of personal freedom**; *Rule of Law* and *Judicial Review* boxes delegated to `Polity/`.
- **Mutual persuasion through reasoned argument appealing to the prevailing value system**; ❌ not each **fighting to secure his self-interest**.
- Autonomy objection; printed reply — autonomy **includes having a share in the decisions that affect oneself and others**.
- Division of labour with professional politicians **retained**, with the duty to **report and justify their decisions and actions to the people regularly** — continuous, answering **every few years**.
- Exponents: **Walzer (1983), Cohen and Rogers (1983), Manin (1987), Hurley (1989), Fishkin (1991)**. ⚠️ **Habermas is not printed here**; one flagged bridge only.

## 10. The four change-concepts and the six transition pairs

- **Revolution**: sudden; causes **mature slowly**, the event **strikes abruptly**. **Evolution**: cumulative, **imperceptible**, simple → complex, **borrowed from biology**.
- **Development**: **stronger, more organized, more efficient, more effective**, **more satisfying in terms of human wants** — **empirical**. **Progress**: **morally superior** — **normative**.
- 🔑 2×2: revolution/evolution on **pace**; development/progress on **measure** → no quantity of development entails progress.
- **Comte** military → industrial; **Spencer** integration → differentiation; **Maine** status → contract; **Tönnies** *Gemeinschaft* → *Gesellschaft*; **Durkheim** mechanical → organic; **Weber** traditional → legal-rational. 📝 Spencer's pair **closely corresponds** to Maine's.

## 11. The dialectic and its three laws

- **Engels, *Anti-Dühring* (1878)**: **humanity's leap from the kingdom of necessity to the kingdom of freedom**. *Theses on Feuerbach* (1845): **the point however is to change it**.
- Hegel's engine, Marx's substrate; **thesis → antithesis → synthesis**; **negation of negation**; **Sabine** — dialectic as a **law of social development**, **struggle** as driver, power **economic rather than political**.
- Law 1 **quantity into quality**: **ice** and **steam** → abruptness from gradual causes.
- Law 2 **interpenetration of opposites**: **hard iron that can be moulded**, **a soft flower that holds its shape** → internal contradiction.
- Law 3 **negation of negation**: **seed → seed-bud → plant → ear of corn**, **in larger quantity and better quality** → advance, not restoration.

## 12. Base, superstructure and the four epochs

- **Forces of production** = means + **labour power**; **relations of production** = **pattern of economic ownership**.
- **1859 Preface**: the real economic structure **on which rises a legal and political superstructure**.
- Religion: **the sob of the oppressed creature, the heart of a heartless world**; **the opium of the people**. ⚠️ Both halves.
- **Fetters** → **Then begins an epoch of social revolution**; **the hand-mill gives you society with the feudal lord, the steam-mill society with the industrial capitalist**.
- Epochs: **primitive communism · ancient slave-owning (master/slave) · medieval feudal (lord/serf) · modern capitalist (bourgeoisie/proletariat)**.
- 🔑 **Sabine**: **1789** was a **political** revolution registering a base already changed.

## 13. Revolution: necessity, finality and the internal dissent

- **Any significant social change is always the product of a revolution**; **revolution is the indispensable midwife of social change**.
- *Class Struggle* and *Ideology* boxes; power never surrendered → **social revolution is the necessary lever of social change**; **1789** as the capitalist order's own founding overthrow.
- Earlier revolutions transfer exploitation; the proletarian is **a revolution of the majority against the minority** → **the final** revolution; socialism → communism **without a further revolution**.
- **Lenin**: **While the State exists, there can be no freedom. When there is freedom there will be no State.**
- 🔑 **Mao's permanent revolution** contradicts finality from inside the tradition.

## 14. Popper, and the two-sided verdict

- **Historicism** (Hegel, Marx, **Spengler**) as **a mask for uncritical attachment to a totalitarian ideology** and a **pseudo-science** because **insulated from falsification**. 🔑 The charge is about **form**.
- *Open Society* (1945): three **false prophets** — Plato's **noble lie**/**royal fable**, Hegel's **march of God on earth**, Marx's class-war myth.
- *Poverty of Historicism* (1957): knowledge grows and **cannot be predicted**; laws only for **fragmented and isolated units**; concentration of capital is **a tendency, not a law**.
- **Methodological individualism**; reframe — not **who should rule** but **how to devise institutions that minimise the risk of bad rulers**.
- **Piecemeal** against **Utopian**: no agreement on a remote ideal → **authoritarian means and violence**; **generational injustice**; **step-by-step approximation**. 📝 Revolution **in the realm of thought** is allowed.
- **Sorel**: **gradual reform never really achieves anything in the long run**. Two-sided caution: no **new privileged class in a new garb**; reform must not **evaporate** and must promote **equality of opportunity** and reduced disparities. **Kafka**: **the slime of a new bureaucracy**.

## 15. The conception of development and the third-world category

- Built for nations independent after **1939–45** → **goal-relative**.
- **More satisfying in terms of human wants and aspirations**; **empirical** scale against **normative** progress.
- Method: identify the **advanced society**, measure distance. 🔑 Step one smuggles in a model.
- **Mittelman (1988)**: **increasing capacity to make rational use of natural and human resources for social ends**; underdevelopment as **the blockage which forestalls a rational transformation of the social structure**.
- **Baran (1957)**: **a far-reaching transformation**… **has never been a smooth, harmonious process**. **Rodney (1974)**: **a many-sided process** — skill, freedom, creativity, **self-discipline**, responsibility, material well-being.
- **Sauvy (1952)**; third-estate parallel; **Western-educated elite**; recurrent military rule; **non-alignment with India a leading member**; the caveat that the term **should have become outdated… yet it still maintains its identity due to long usage**.

## 16. Two explanations of persistent underdevelopment

- **Liberal**: stages; **differentiation**; narrow-group → national loyalty; **ascribed → achieved**; legitimacy; **Bryce (1921), Friedrich (1937), Almond and Coleman (1960), Almond and Powell (1966), Pye (1966)**; open trade and market reliance.
- **Marxist**: **Lenin (1916)** — the **proletariat of the international struggle**; **Baran (1957)** — colonial accumulation unrepeatable; 🔑 **Frank (1967) centre–periphery / metropolis–satellite**, one process at two ends, **surviving political independence** → **delinking**; **Rodney**, **Cumings**, **Amin (1974)**; **Unequal Exchange**.
- ⚠️ The departure: explanation runs **between** societies. Tenet: underdevelopment is **not a stage** but a **condition of domination**; the **primary-product trap**; the **cartel** prescription with **OPEC (1960)**.
- Conclusion: **south–south cooperation**; **borrowed plus indigenous technology** (Western technology suits **an affluent, highly motivated and small population**); a **modicum** of competition without **cut-throat competition**; welfare to **build human resources**, **lest they generate vested interests of the incompetent and indolent**; no **luxury of consumerism**; a possible **blend of material and spiritual values**.

## 17. The market-society path and its three criticisms

- **Development = modernization = Westernization**; **Coleman's modern society** — **urbanisation, widespread literacy, high per-capita income, geographical and social mobility, commercialisation and industrialisation, penetrative mass communication, widespread participation**.
- **Rostow's five**: traditional · preconditions for take-off · take-off · drive to maturity · high mass consumption. **Organski's four**: primitive unification · industrialisation · national welfare · politics of abundance.
- 🔑 **Frank (1971)**: Western underdevelopment **original**, developing-country underdevelopment a **consequence** — **How can they follow the same pattern of development when their starting points are radically different?**
- Communitarian objection: loss of the **modicum of social solidarity** that compensated for material poverty. Profit objection: **higher education, art and culture** geared to **market needs**; people **reduced to self-seeking animals**.
- Trade-off: welfare gained **at the expense of their moral strength**.

## 18. The welfare path, from Bismarck to Beveridge

- A **modified liberal** position.
- 🔑 **Bismarck's "state socialism"** — introduced by a chancellor **opposed to both liberalism and socialism**, to **counter the appeal of socialism**.
- **Asquith**, **National Insurance Act (1911)**; **Beveridge Report (1942)** — free **national health service**, **family allowances**, **full employment**, universal insurance **from the cradle to the grave** covering unemployment, sickness, accident, old age, widows' pensions, funeral grants, maternity; **progressive taxation**.
- 🔑 **Poor-relief stigma** removed; assistance as **mutual assistance and self-reliance**; the **United States** the printed exception.
- Developing-country verdict: **almost indispensable** as the **traditional support base** erodes, yet **only on a subdued scale**, blunted by **bureaucratic inefficiency and corruption** → a ceiling of **capacity, not principle**.

## 19. The socialist and Gandhian paths, and the four-model table

- **Revolutionary socialism**: class struggle, **full-scale socialization**; **almost universal employment, education and health care**, **although they hardly care to protect civil liberties and political rights**.
- **Evolutionary/democratic socialism**: rights first, social rights progressive → **merges with the welfare-state model**; **England since the 1940s**; **India emulating it**; decline through population, shortage, bureaucratic inefficiency and **the mad rush of demands for subsidies and reservations** → **liberalisation, privatisation and globalisation**, with the warning against **all-pervasive commercialization**.
- 🔑 **Gandhian**: a rival **criterion**. *Young India* (1927) — **I call it satanic**; improvement lies **in his conscience**; **need but not their greed**; **bread labour**; *Harijan* (1935) — **dead machinery** against the **millions of living machines** in the **seven hundred thousand villages**; **production by the masses**; non-violence extended to **nature**; **Machinery has its place; it has come to stay. But it must not be allowed to displace necessary human labour.**
- Four-model table rows: status of the individual · entitlements · status of rights · attitude to industrialisation · **attitude toward nature** · goal. 🔑 Three **Indifferent**, Gandhian **Very friendly**.

## 20. The ecological correction

- Development is **primarily a positive phenomenon** turning negative in the **economic** sphere; arc from replenishable extraction to **depletion** and **contamination**.
- **Brundtland, *Our Common Future* (1987)** — present needs without compromising future generations; ⚠️ paraphrased; **needs** tied to poverty eradication.
- ✅ The **1972 Stockholm** meeting is the **UN Conference on the Human Environment**. ❌ Never "Earth Summit, Stockholm 1972".
- **Environmentalism**: humans from **masters** to **partners**, requiring **a thorough-going change in the organization of human world itself**; the **humility** argument.
- **Schumacher (1973)**: **consumes the very basis on which it has been erected**; **eating away its own capital… digging its own grave**; 🔑 recorded income is partly **capital consumption**; **small-scale, self-governing communities** — **Small is beautiful**.
- ⚠️ **Trainer's** consumption figures: reported, dated 1985, ❌ never current. Population argument given **symmetrically**.
- Slogans: **borrowed it from our children**; **Think globally, act locally**; **Neither left nor right, but forwards**.

## 21. Political development, the two buildings and the sequencing refutation

- Political development = institutions able to **meet changing needs**; derived **from the liberal tradition** → **political modernization**.
- **Coleman–Pye**: **differentiation** (occupational from kinship, legal from religion, administration from politics) · **equality**, **the ethos of modernity**, **subjects** becoming **citizens** · **capacity** = the **development syndrome**.
- **Almond–Powell (1966)**: input — **socialisation, articulation, aggregation, communication**; output — **rule-making, rule-application, rule-adjudication**; **parochial → subject → participant**; capabilities **regulative, extractive, distributive, responsive**.
- 🔑 **The balance rule**: command and collection must be **matched** by delivery and answerability.
- Anti-convergence: combine **with its own genius**; ❌ not **reduced to competitive market societies in the name of their political development**.
- **Nation-building** = loyalty transferred, producing identity, allegiance, emotional bond, solidarity. **State-building** = a **common centre of power** extending law, order, defence and welfare to **every part** of the territory. **Complementary and simultaneous**.
- 🔑 **Kothari (1976)**: **European history does not corroborate** the modernise-first thesis, since consolidation **preceded** modernisation → **precedence to state-building and nation-building**. This is the last argument in the source.

## 22. Post-source Core extension — labelled, conceptual, entity-free

- **Populism**: virtuous **people** against corrupt **elite**; **moralisation · homogenisation · anti-institutionalism**; **thin-centred ideology** (**Mudde, 2004**) against **logic of articulation** (**Laclau, 2005**); vertical against horizontal boundary; case for — symptom of representational failure, re-politicisation, participation, agenda power; case against — **exclusive representation** (**Müller, 2016**) → illegitimate opposition, unprotected minorities, **institutional hollowing**.
- 🔑 Popular democracy **multiplies** channels; populism **collapses** them — the premise that the people is **not plural** is the divider. ⚠️ A leader-centred anti-elite movement invites **Michels's iron law**.
- **Democratisation**: liberalisation → contestation → institutionalisation → consolidation; elections **necessary, insufficient**. **Backsliding**: incremental weakening **within** an order still holding elections. **Social movements**: conditional democratic value.
- **Ecological political theory ≠ the sustainable-development model**: **anthropocentric / biocentric / ecocentric** standing; the **productivism** critique; **intergenerational justice**; stewardship and environmental justice; the **eco-authoritarian** risk stated fairly and answered by failed premise, legitimacy as a condition of effectiveness, deliberation's fit, and **just transition**.
- **Digital democracy**, democratic-theory layer only: consent (differential information, speed of falsehood, thin terms), representation (**disintermediation**, fragmented address, with a real inclusion gain), public sphere (ownership, **attention** as the scarce good, fragmentation, loss of a common agenda). ⚠️ The historical public sphere was itself exclusive.
- 📰 One dated anchor: **India AI Impact Summit 2026**, Bharat Mandapam, New Delhi, **19–20 February 2026**, three *sutras* **People · Planet · Progress** — enrichment only, used once, ❌ no figure or classification drawn from it.

## 23. The assembled synthesis, and the close of the subject

- ⚠️ No printed section answers the democracy-and-development question; it is assembled across six clusters.
- Step 0: refuse the framing; fix the conception. Step 1: **empirical** development against **normative** progress; **capacity · structure · person**. 🔑 Step 2: the constitutive move — democracy is **partly constitutive** of development so defined.
- Four axes: participation and responsiveness; substantive against procedural; the path chosen; sequencing.
- Verdict conditions: participation **channelled**; responsive and distributive capability **matched** to regulative and extractive; procedure accompanied by material attention; state-building and nation-building not deferred.
- Failure modes: mobilisation replacing reasoned claim; procedure treated as sufficient; one path adopted as the only criterion; ecological cost excluded from measurement.
- ❌ No country named as an instance. ❌ No forward pointer: **the subject ends here.**

## Scope, typing and provenance

- A **Mains-focused** edition for GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay, and a **standalone session for Topic 23 alone**. It is not merged with any neighbouring topic and does not absorb one. Depth and format drop to Mains level; the topic's own syllabus share does not.
- Political Theory is **supplementary conceptual support**. No General Studies or Optional previous-year question is claimed, implied or manufactured; every verified stem is labelled at each use with its true owner.
- **Page anchors.** The source is cited as `PDF p.N (printed M)` on a flat offset of **19** across pp.513–575. ⚠️ No offset is extrapolated beyond the cited range, and no claim is made about other editions or printings. Owner-side material, the post-source extension at Lesson 22 and the single current anchor carry **no** page anchor, by design.
- **Typing.** Unmarked statements carrying a page anchor are drawn from the three chapters. ⚠️ marks inference, synthesis or a flagged limitation; 🔶 marks material owned by this topic's canonical files rather than by the book; ✅ marks a verified fact or a required correction; ❌ marks a trap or a prohibition; 📝 marks a note on printed wording, attribution or a scan artefact; 🔑 marks the highest-value item in a block; ➡️ marks material deferred to another topic or subject; 📰 marks a dated current item. **Bold is not a quotation marker** — it also carries emphasis, headings and labels, and the diagrams and register notes paraphrase as well as reproduce.
- **Provenance upgrade applied.** The Basic owner's §12 heading and the provisional tags on its **Sartori** and **Aron** entries are upgraded here, because both are printed at pp.515–516. ⚠️ Provisional status is retained only for Sartori's *The Theory of Democracy Revisited* (1987), Mosca's "political formula" label, the C. Wright Mills bridge and the Habermas bridge.
- **Quotation discipline.** Material reproduced as the chapter's own wording is confined to the verbatim-safe set certified in the frozen audit — Marx and Engels, Lenin, Lefebvre, Maguire, MacIver, the 1859 Preface, *Capital* Vol.3, Sabine, Schumpeter's definition, Mannheim, Macpherson, Norman Barry, Gandhi, Schumacher, Coleman's modern-society definition, Mittelman, Baran, Rodney, Fosdick, Disraeli, Tennyson, Kafka, Trainer and the three environmental slogans. ❌ **No quotation is invented** for Mosca, Pareto, Michels, Aron, Sartori, Bentley, Truman, Latham, Dahl, Plamenatz, Gramsci, Popper, Sorel, Rostow, Organski, Bismarck, Asquith, Beveridge, Pye, Almond, Powell or Kothari; each is paraphrased as proposition. The same rule governs every name in the post-source extension, where titles and years are cited without quotation.
- **Presentational normalisations, disclosed.** Typographic quotation marks and apostrophes are rendered as straight ASCII; line-break hyphenation is rejoined; running heads and folios are removed from reproduced material; and sentences running across a page boundary are rejoined — notably the Marxist critique at PDF 524–525, the transitional-phase passage at 526–527, the Popper appraisal at 553–554, the development definitions at 555–556 and the political-development comparison at 573–574. ⚠️ It is therefore not true that no spacing or layout was altered.

## Open uncertainties
